# Verify proposed fixes to LR explanations

Another worker proposed edits to LSAT U's LR Question Explanations. You are the independent check. Nothing is applied unless you approve it. Be skeptical: your job is to catch edits that are wrong, not to admire them.

## Inputs
- The proposals: a JSON list at the path you are given. Each has qid, table, row_id, column, style, letter (for notes), old, new, kind, why, evidence.
- Read-only mirror of the database: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/` (`lr_questions.json` has stimulus, prompt = stem, correct_choice; `lr_answer_choices.json` has choice_text, tag per letter; `lr_explanations.json`, `lr_explanation_steps.json`, `lr_explanation_choice_notes.json`).
- The bank students read: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu/public/data/PT{n}-S{s}-LR.json`.
Load JSON with Python (`python -I`), never whole files into context. Write scripts only in your work directory.

## For every proposal
Read the question's stimulus, stem, all five choices, the key, and the old and new text in full. Then decide:
- **approve** — the new text is correct: every quote matches the choice or stimulus exactly, the logic is right for this question and key, the verdict for the letter agrees with the key (correct letter is argued as correct, wrong letters as wrong), nothing outside the defect was damaged, and it reads clearly.
- **fix** — the edit is the right idea but has a mistake you can correct. Give the full corrected `new`.
- **reject** — the edit is wrong, unnecessary, or makes things worse. Say why.

Hard rules for approve/fix: no added em dashes; no added gendered pronouns (he, she, his, her...) for people in the stimulus; no mention of Logic Games; never change which choice is credited; a wrong-answer note must say why that choice is wrong, a correct-answer note why it is right.

Also settle any open question the proposer raised in its `why` (for example a disputed verdict). If you cannot settle it with confidence, reject that edit and say what is needed.

## Output
Write JSON Lines to the output path you are given, one per proposal, in input order:
`{"row_id": "...", "qid": "...", "verdict": "approve|fix|reject", "new": "<only when fix: full corrected text>", "note": "one sentence"}`

Reply with counts of approve, fix, reject, and list every reject and fix with its one-sentence reason.

61	=== PT158-S2-Q22 type=Parallel Flaw key=C
62	STIM: Devan has never bothered to be kind to me. Nor has he offered help or companionship. So, since he does not meet any of these basic criteria for friendship, he is my enemy.
63	STEM: Which one of the following exhibits flawed reasoning most similar to the flawed reasoning exhibited in the argument above?
64	  (A) [Different Flaw] Each officer of this club must be a member of two years standing, or be a committee member, or have special qualifications. Evelyn cannot be an officer, for she has only been a member for one year and is not a committee member.
65	  (B) [Valid Logic] In order to thrive, this plant needs to be located in a sunny spot and to be watered regularly. So, since this spot is not sunny, and since I did not water this plant regularly, that explains why this plant is not as healthy as it should be.
66	  (C) [Correct] This book has been widely reviewed and hasn't received even one hostile review. Hence we can conclude that, so far, all the critics have loved this book.
67	  (D) [Different Flaw] A decision in favor of developing the northern border of the town logically implies that it would be equally acceptable to develop the southern, eastern, or western borders. So, it is possible that at least one of these other borders will also be developed.
68	  (E) [Different Flaw] If everyone were an author, poet, or academic, then society would come crashing to a halt. But most people are not poets or authors. Nor are there many academics in our society. Therefore, our society will not come crashing to a halt.
69	 -- explanation style=standard
70	   step0 [Conclusion (So)] Devan is my enemy.
71	   step1 [Evidence (since)] He does not meet any of these basic criteria for friendship (being kind / offering to help / being compassionate).
72	   step2 [Evaluate] How would we object to this conclusion. Given that Devan has never been kind, offered help, or showed compassion, how could we argue that he is not our enemy? Isn't there some middle ground between "not being a friend" and "being an enemy"? Those aren't binary options. It feels like the author is imposing some False Choice that if someone isn't a friend, they must be an enemy. Maybe Devan is just a stranger or just a neutral party, who isn't necessarily for us but isn't against us.
73	   step3 [Goal] Let's look for an argument whose conclusion feels like it's swinging to the opposite pole from what the evidence is talking about. i.e. Jim doesn't meet any of the criteria for "best employee". Thus, he is the worst employee.
74	   note A: This argument has a different logical structure from the stimulus. It establishes requirements for being a club officer (two years' standing OR committee member) and concludes that someone who meets neither requirement is not an officer. This is actually valid reasoning — if the requirements are genuinely necessary conditions, failing all of them does disqualify someone. The flaw in the stimulus was jumping from "fails friendship criteria" to "is an enemy" — a false dichotomy between two extreme categories. This answer concludes only that someone is not an officer, which is the direct logical consequence of failing the criteria. There is no leap to an extreme opposite category. It concludes "not-X" from failing the criteria for X, whereas the stimulus concluded "anti-X" from failing the criteria for X.
75	   note B: This argument is logically valid, not flawed. It states that a plant needs sunny location AND regular watering to thrive. The plant is not in a sunny spot, so at least one necessary condition is unmet. The conclusion — that the plant will not thrive — follows logically. This is straightforward modus tollens: if both A and B are necessary for C, and A is absent, then C will not occur. The stimulus's flaw was a false dichotomy — leaping from "not friend" to "enemy." This answer contains no such leap; it simply concludes that a necessary condition is unmet, therefore the outcome will not occur. There is no jump to an opposite extreme category.
76	   note C: This answer perfectly mirrors the stimulus's flawed reasoning. The book has been widely reviewed and received no hostile reviews, so it does not meet the criteria for a "bad" book. The conclusion then leaps to: therefore it must be a masterpiece. This is the same false dichotomy as the stimulus. Just as the stimulus jumped from "not a friend" to "enemy" (ignoring mediocre, average, or indifferent relationships), this answer jumps from "not bad" (no hostile reviews) to "masterpiece" (extreme positive), ignoring the vast middle ground of books that are merely decent, competent, forgettable, or mediocre. The structure is identical: failing to meet the criteria for one extreme category, therefore belonging to the opposite extreme category, with all intermediate categories completely overlooked. A book with no hostile reviews could simply be unremarkable — just as someone who is not a friend could simply be a stranger.
77	   note D: This argument says a decision to develop the northern border logically implies it is not in the town's best interest to use the land for recreation. This is a different type of flaw — it assumes that choosing one use for land automatically means an alternative use is not in the town's interest. The flaw here is about conflating a decision with a judgment about the best interest, or about opportunity cost and land use exclusivity. The stimulus's flaw is a false dichotomy between two extreme relational categories (friend/enemy). This answer does not leap from "not-X" to "opposite of X" — it draws an inference about competing uses of a resource. The logical structures are different: the stimulus treats two categories as exhaustive, while this answer treats two land uses as mutually exclusive and draws an evaluative conclusion about one from the selection of the other.
78	   note E: This argument claims that if everyone belonged to certain intellectual professions, society would collapse. This is a conditional claim about a hypothetical universal scenario. The structure is entirely different from the stimulus. The stimulus identifies specific criteria not met by an individual and then classifies that individual in an extreme opposite category. This answer makes a prediction about a hypothetical world where everyone has the same profession. There is no "not-X therefore anti-X" structure, no false dichotomy between two extreme categories, and no ignored middle ground. The flaw, if any, is about the unrealistic hypothetical or about overlooking that society can have some authors without everyone being one. The logical pattern does not match.
79	 -- explanation style=brief
80	   step0 [Conclusion] Devan is my enemy.
81	   step1 [Evidence] Devan has not been kind, has not offered help, and has not offered companionship. He fails to meet any basic criteria for friendship.
82	   step2 [The Flaw] The argument assumes that failing to qualify as a friend automatically makes someone an enemy. This is a false dichotomy — there is a middle ground (acquaintance, stranger, indifferent person) between friend and enemy.
83	   step3 [Goal] Find the answer that mirrors this exact structure: failing to meet criteria for Category A, therefore belongs to opposite Category B — ignoring intermediate categories.
84	   note A: This is actually valid: failing the requirements for being an officer means you are not an officer. The stimulus's flaw was leaping from "not a friend" to "an enemy." Here, the conclusion is simply "not an officer" — no false dichotomy.
85	   note B: This is valid reasoning: the plant needs sun and water to thrive, it lacks sun, so it will not thrive. No false dichotomy, no leap to an opposite category. Just sound modus tollens.
86	   note C: No hostile reviews (not bad) therefore masterpiece (best possible). Same false dichotomy as the stimulus: not meeting criteria for one extreme, so it must be the opposite extreme. Both ignore the vast middle ground.
87	   note D: This is about conflating a land-use decision with a judgment about best interests — a different flaw from the false dichotomy between friend and enemy in the stimulus.
88	   note E: A hypothetical about everyone being intellectuals causing societal collapse is structurally different from the stimulus's false dichotomy between friend and enemy. No "not-X therefore anti-X" pattern exists here.
89	 -- explanation style=another_way
90	   step0 [Conclusion] Devan is officially classified as an enemy. Break out the nemesis paperwork.
91	   step1 [Evidence] Devan has not been kind, helpful, or companionable. Zero for three on the friendship checklist. Therefore — enemy.
92	   step2 [The Flaw] This is the "you are either with me or against me" fallacy. Just because someone is not your friend does not make them your enemy. They could be a stranger, an acquaintance, the person who sits two rows behind you in class that you have never spoken to. There is a vast middle ground between "best buddy" and "arch-nemesis," and this argument leapfrogged right over it.
93	   step3 [Goal] Find the answer with the same false dichotomy: something does not qualify for one category, so it must belong to the extreme opposite category. Same leap, same missed middle ground.
94	   note A: This argument says: "Does not meet the requirements for club officer, so not a club officer." That is just... correct. Failing the requirements means you do not get the job. The original argument said "does not meet friendship criteria, so ENEMY" — a massive logical leap from "not qualified for A" to "automatically in the worst possible category B." This answer just concludes "not qualified for A, so not A." No absurd leap. No false dichotomy. Just basic logic working as intended.
95	   note B: Plant needs sun and water. Plant has no sun. Plant will not thrive. This is just... correct reasoning. There is no flaw to parallel because there is no flaw. It would be like trying to find a car accident that matches another car accident, and someone points at a car driving perfectly safely through a green light. Cannot match a flaw that does not exist.
96	   note C: "No one said it was terrible, so it must be a masterpiece!" — same energy as "Devan was not nice to me, so he must be my sworn enemy!" Both arguments looked at one end of the spectrum, found no evidence for it, and catapulted themselves to the opposite extreme. A book with no hostile reviews might just be aggressively mediocre — so bland that nobody bothered to hate it. A person who is not your friend might just be the barista who makes your coffee. Both arguments skipped the enormous middle ground between "bad" and "best" (or "friend" and "enemy") like it did not exist. Perfect parallel.
97	   note D: "We chose to build houses on the land, so a park must not be in our interest." That is not the same mistake as "Devan is not my friend, so he is my enemy." The first is about confusing a practical decision with a value judgment — you can think parks are great and still choose to build houses. The second is about leaping from one extreme to the opposite. Different logical errors, different aisles in the flaw supermarket.
98	   note E: "If everyone were a poet, society would collapse!" This is a completely different kind of argument from "Devan is not nice, so he is my enemy." One is a dramatic hypothetical about what would happen if the whole world changed. The other is a snap judgment about one person based on a false dichotomy. They are not even in the same logical zip code. It is like trying to match a speeding ticket with a parking violation because they both involve cars.

172	=== PT158-S3-Q2 type=Strengthen key=C
173	STIM: Educator: Few problems faced in daily life can be solved most effectively, if at all, by applying knowledge from any single academic discipline in isolation. Thus, schools should not require students to take courses in individual academic disciplines but should instead require them to take interdisciplinary courses.
174	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the educator's argument?
175	  (A) [No Impact] Problems faced in daily life usually can be solved effectively using only common sense.
176	  (B) [Weakens] Most teachers are able to teach courses in a single academic discipline more effectively than they can teach interdisciplinary courses.
177	  (C) [Correct] Students who take only courses in individual academic disciplines are rarely able to combine knowledge from those disciplines.
178	  (D) [Weakens] Most students who are required to take courses that cover only single disciplines can effectively solve many problems faced in daily life.
179	  (E) [Weakens, If Anything] Most interdisciplinary courses are not designed specifically to teach students how to solve problems faced in daily life.
180	 -- explanation style=standard
181	   step0 [Conclusion (Thus)] Schools should not require students to take courses in individual academic disciplines but should instead require them to take interdisciplinary courses.
182	   step1 [Evidence] Most problems in daily life can't be solved most effectively, if at all, by applying knowledge from any single academic discipline in isolation.
183	   step2 [Evaluate] We could spell out the Linking assumption here, If most problems can't be solved most effectively by applying knowledge from any single academic discipline in isolation, then schools shouldn't require students to take classes in single academic disciplines. But it seems more likely that the correct answer will be Defender style, ruling out some objection. GIven that ... most problems in daily life can't be solved best by applying knowledge from any single discipline. How could we still argue that ... schools should require students to take courses in individual disciplines? We could maybe say that solving problems in daily life involves applying knowledge from multiple disciplines, but it's too chaotic and disorganized to learn in that interdisciplinary style in school. Maybe it's better to require courses in individual academic disciplines, and then in daily life when these students face problems, they can just combine things they learned from these separate disciplines.
184	   step3 [Goal] Look for an answer like, "it is not easy to combine things you've learned from individual disciplines in order to face problems in daily life", or an answer linking the premise to the conclusion, or any other answer that, if negated, would hurt the argument.
185	   note A: If common sense alone can solve most daily-life problems, this actually undermines the entire premise of the educator's argument. The educator's reasoning depends on the idea that daily-life problems require multi-disciplinary academic knowledge. If common sense handles most of those problems, then the need for any particular course structure — single-discipline or interdisciplinary — becomes questionable. But this answer does not strengthen the argument; if anything, it makes the entire debate about course structure less relevant. The educator's recommendation is motivated by the difficulty of daily problems; if those problems are not actually that hard, the motivation evaporates regardless of whether you restructure the courses.
186	   note B: If teachers are better at teaching single-discipline courses, this creates a practical reason to keep the current system. The educator argues that interdisciplinary courses would better prepare students for daily life, but this answer introduces a significant cost: teaching quality would decline under the proposed change. Better teaching in single-discipline courses means students may actually learn more effectively from the existing format, even if the format does not perfectly mirror daily-life problem structures. This answer gives a reason to prefer the status quo rather than to adopt the educator's recommendation, which pushes against the conclusion rather than supporting it.
187	   note C: The educator's argument has a critical gap: it assumes that because daily-life problems require multi-disciplinary knowledge, the way courses are structured determines whether students can apply that knowledge effectively. This answer bridges that gap directly. If students who take only single-discipline courses are rarely able to combine knowledge from different disciplines on their own, then the course format is the bottleneck — not the students' ability or motivation. This means switching to interdisciplinary courses is not merely a theoretical improvement; it addresses a real, demonstrated failure of the current system. Students are not naturally acquiring cross-disciplinary integration skills from separate courses, so the courses themselves need to build that capability. This provides exactly the bridge premise the argument was missing and makes the conclusion significantly more compelling.
188	   note D: If most students who take only single-discipline courses can effectively combine knowledge from different fields, that directly undermines the need for interdisciplinary courses. The educator's argument depends on the idea that single-discipline instruction leaves students unable to integrate across fields. But this answer says students handle the integration just fine on their own. If students already possess the cross-disciplinary competence the educator wants to build, then restructuring the courses is unnecessary. This answer removes the very problem the educator's proposal is designed to solve, which weakens rather than strengthens the conclusion.
189	   note E: If most interdisciplinary courses are not designed to teach students how to solve daily-life problems, this undermines the educator's recommendation. The educator's entire reasoning is that daily-life problems require multi-disciplinary approaches, so courses should be interdisciplinary. But if the available interdisciplinary courses do not actually target daily-life problem-solving, then switching to them would not achieve the educator's stated goal. This introduces a practical problem with the proposed solution: the courses that exist do not match the purpose for which they are being recommended. At best this answer is irrelevant to whether the switch should happen in principle; at worst it suggests the switch would be ineffective in practice.
190	 -- explanation style=brief
191	   step0 [Conclusion] Schools should require interdisciplinary courses instead of courses in individual academic disciplines.
192	   step1 [Evidence] Few daily-life problems can be solved most effectively by applying knowledge from a single academic discipline in isolation.
193	   step2 [Evaluate] The argument assumes that interdisciplinary courses will actually equip students to combine knowledge from multiple disciplines. If students in single-discipline courses can already integrate knowledge on their own, the argument weakens. If they cannot, the argument strengthens.
194	   step3 [Goal] Find an answer that bridges the gap between "daily problems require multiple disciplines" and "therefore schools should teach interdisciplinary courses" — ideally by showing that single-discipline courses fail to produce the needed cross-disciplinary competence.
195	   note A: If common sense solves most daily problems, the educator's entire premise weakens. The need for interdisciplinary courses depends on academic knowledge being necessary, and this answer suggests it is not.
196	   note B: If teachers are more effective at teaching single-discipline courses, that is a practical reason to keep the current system. This undercuts rather than supports the switch to interdisciplinary instruction.
197	   note C: This bridges the key gap: single-discipline courses leave students unable to combine knowledge across fields. Since daily problems require cross-disciplinary knowledge and students cannot develop that integration on their own, interdisciplinary courses become necessary.
198	   note D: If students already combine knowledge effectively from single-discipline courses, there is no need to switch to interdisciplinary courses. This removes the problem the educator's proposal is trying to fix.
199	   note E: If interdisciplinary courses are not designed to teach daily-life problem-solving, switching to them would not achieve the educator's goal. This undermines the recommendation rather than supporting it.
200	 -- explanation style=another_way
201	   step0 [Conclusion] The educator's prescription: ditch the single-discipline courses and go interdisciplinary.
202	   step1 [Evidence] Real-life problems do not respect departmental boundaries. They require knowledge from multiple fields, so a biology-only or history-only course does not match the demands of actual daily life.
203	   step2 [Evaluate] Fair point about the problems, but there is a missing step. Just because daily life requires combined knowledge does not automatically mean schools need to pre-blend the courses. Maybe students who take separate biology and chemistry courses can still figure out how to combine the knowledge themselves. The argument assumes that course structure determines student capability, but it never proves that connection. A good strengthener will show that students actually cannot make the leap on their own — that taking separate courses leaves them unable to integrate across fields.
204	   step3 [Goal] Find the answer that closes the gap between "life needs multiple disciplines" and "courses should be interdisciplinary."
205	   note A: "Just use common sense" is the argument-killer here, not the argument-strengthener. If most daily problems can be solved without any academic knowledge at all, then who cares whether the courses are interdisciplinary or single-discipline? The whole debate becomes pointless. The educator needs daily problems to be hard enough to require academic knowledge but too complex for one discipline alone. This answer says they are not that hard in the first place.
206	   note B: Great idea, one problem: the teachers are worse at it. If instructors deliver better education in single-discipline courses, then switching to interdisciplinary formats could mean students actually learn less. It is like recommending that everyone drive an all-terrain vehicle because life has bumpy roads, but then revealing that none of the driving instructors know how to teach all-terrain driving. The theory might be sound, but the implementation tanks it.
207	   note C: This is the missing puzzle piece. The educator's argument had a gap big enough to drive a school bus through: maybe students who take separate biology and chemistry courses can figure out on their own how to combine the knowledge. This answer slams that door shut. Students who take only single-discipline courses rarely manage to combine the knowledge — they have all the ingredients but cannot cook the meal. That means the course structure actually matters, and swapping to interdisciplinary courses is not just a nice idea but a solution to a real, demonstrated problem.
208	   note D: This answer basically says "students are already doing fine — they take separate courses and figure out how to put the knowledge together." If that is true, the educator's entire argument collapses. Why redesign the curriculum to fix a problem that does not exist? Students are already cooking the cross-disciplinary meal with single-discipline ingredients. This is the opposite of what the educator needs to hear.
209	   note E: The educator says "switch to interdisciplinary courses because life requires it." This answer fires back: "those interdisciplinary courses were not built for real-life problem-solving anyway." So the recommended fix does not actually address the identified problem. It is like a doctor diagnosing a vitamin deficiency and then prescribing a pill that does not contain that vitamin. The logic of the diagnosis might be fine, but the prescription misses the mark.

If students already have the problem-solving ability the educator wants to build, then restructuring the courses looks unnecessary. 35	STIM: Anthropologist: Many people think that if human language evolved, then something like it must be present in those species most closely related to humans, such as chimpanzees. They reason that since new traits evolve gradually, something like human language, albeit cruder, must exist in some species from which humans evolved. This general line of argument may be reasonable, but it simply does not follow that chimpanzees have anything like human language, because humans did not evolve from chimpanzees. While chimpanzees are indeed closely related to humans, this is because both evolved from a common ancestor. The evolution of human language might easily have begun after the extinction of that common ancestor.

39	  (C) [Opposing Point] The communicative systems of chimpanzees are cruder than human language.
40	  (D) [Out of Scope] Human language is a by-product of human intelligence, which chimpanzees lack.
41	  (E) [Evidence / Too Strong] The evolution of human language began after the disappearance of an extinct species from which both humans and chimpanzees evolved.

302	=== PT148-S3-Q19 type=Agree/Disagree key=C
303	STIM: Ana: On libertarian principles, I oppose the proposed smoking ban. It is not the government's business to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves.
304	
305	Pankaj: But keep in mind that the ban would apply only to smoking in public places. People could still smoke all they want in private.
306	STEM: The dialogue provides the most support for the claim that Ana and Pankaj disagree over whether
307	  (A) [Out of Scope Person 2] it is the government's business to prevent people from harming themselves
308	  (B) [Out of Scope Person 2] government should be restrained by libertarian principles
309	  (C) [Correct] the proposed smoking ban is intended to prevent harm only to smokers themselves
310	  (D) [Unknown Person 1] the proposed ban would prohibit smoking in public places
311	  (E) [Out of Scope Person 2] there are cases in which government should attempt to regulate private behavior
312	 -- explanation style=single
313	   step0 [Ana's 1st Claim] On libertarian, principles, I oppose the proposed smoking ban.
314	*Does Pankaj say he does not oppose the smoking ban?*
315	
316	No, nothing definitive. He pushes back against Ana's opposition, but that doesn't necessarily mean that he supports the ban. On LSAT, it's common for someone to disagree with person A's rationale for reaching position X, even though they don't necessarily disagree with position X.
317	   step1 [Ana's 2nd Claim] It isn't the government's business to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves.
318	*Does Pankaj say he thinks that it* **is** *the government's business sometimes to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves?*
319	
320	No, he doesn't say anything about the government or what its role is.
321	   step2 [Evaluate] Since Pankaj didn't disagree with any of Ana's explicit claims, we should start asking ourselves what Ana was *assuming*, because maybe Pankaj is disagreeing with an assumption.
322	
323	The connection between Ana's two sentences assumes that "the proposed smoking ban *is trying to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves*".
324	
325	Does Pankaj disagree with this?
326	
327	Yes! He's saying that people are still allowed to smoke all they want in private (it's not preventing people from doing things that harm only themselves). The ban applies only to smoking public spaces (so it's aimed at preventing people from doing things that harm *others*, in this case preventing cigarette smokers from harming others with their secondhand smoke).
328	   step3 [Goal] Look for something like "Whether the ban is preventing people from doing things that harm only themselves".
329	   note A: As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.
330	   note B: As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.
331	   note C: Ana would agree with this. It's implied by her statements that she opposes the ban because she thinks it's intended to prevent harm only to smokers themselves and she rejects that on libertarian principles.
332	
333	Panjak would disagree with this, because by pointing out that you are banned from smoking in public places but not in private, he's implying that the ban is targeting the effects of smoking on others not on the smoker him/herself.
334	   note D: Pankaj would clearly agree with this because he explicitly says this. Can we derive from Ana's paragraph that she thinks that "the proposed ban would not prohibit smoking in public places"?
335	
336	No, we can't derive that. She may think that the smoking ban applies to public places (as well as private places).
337	
338	If we changed this answer to say "the proposed ban would prohibit smoking only in public places", then we could probably pick it.
339	   note E: As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.
340

2	STIM: In grasslands near the Namib Desert there are "fairy circles"—large, circular patches that are entirely devoid of vegetation. Since sand termite colonies were found in every fairy circle they investigated, scientists hypothesize that it is the burrowing activities of these termites that cause the circles to form.

7	  (D) [No Impact] Fairy circles tend to form in areas that already have numerous other fairy circles.
8	  (E) [No Impact] Species of animals that feed on sand termites are often found living near fairy circles.

28	=== PT158-S2-Q16 type=Paradox key=C
29	STIM: Historians have found that significant intellectual advances generally occur in cities or countries that have a stable political system. But these same historians acknowledge that in ancient Athens, Plato and Aristotle made unparalleled intellectual progress during a period of great political and social unrest.
30	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to reconcile the historians' apparent counterexample with their general rule?
31	  (A) [No Impact] The political systems that have emerged since the time of Plato and Aristotle have in various ways been different from the political system in ancient Athens.
32	  (B) [No Impact] The citizens of ancient Athens generally held in high esteem people who were accomplished intellectually.
33	  (C) [Correct] Financial support for intellectual endeavors is typically unavailable in unstable political environments, but in ancient Athens such support was provided by wealthy citizens.
34	  (D) [No Impact] Significant intellectual advances sometimes, though not always, lead to stable political environments.
35	  (E) [No Impact] Many thinkers besides Plato and Aristotle contributed to the intellectual achievements of ancient Athens.
36	 -- explanation style=standard
37	   step0 [Given that ...] Significant intellectual advances generally occur in cities or countries that have a stable political system.
38	   step1 [How can it be that ...] Plato and Aristotle made unparalleled intellectual progress during a period of great political and social unrest?
39	   step2 [Evaluate / Goal] This isn't really paradoxical, since the general rule in the first sentence is only saying that intellectual advances generally occur in stable political systems. That's not saying that advances only occur in stable political systems. So the fact that Plato and Aristotle are examples of advances that occurred in unstable political systems isn't really contradicting the general rule. It's just an exception. So we should probably understand our task as, "how come Plato and Aristotle were able to make their intellectual advances, despite the unstable political system?" (Maybe they were well-insulated from instability and so within the world of Plato's Academy it still felt like a stable political system)
40	   note A: Knowing that modern political systems vary from those of Plato's time does not help explain how Plato and Aristotle achieved intellectual advances during political instability. This answer provides a general historical observation about the evolution of political systems without identifying any specific mechanism that would reconcile the counterexample with the rule. The paradox asks: why did instability not prevent intellectual progress in Athens? The fact that political systems have changed since then offers no insight into what feature of ancient Athens allowed intellectual work to flourish despite turmoil. It is background information that fails to address the specific puzzle.
41	   note B: The citizens of Athens holding intellectuals in high esteem explains why intellectual work might have been socially rewarded, but it does not address the specific relationship between political instability and intellectual output. The general rule is that instability prevents intellectual advances — presumably because instability disrupts the conditions that make intellectual work possible (funding, safety, institutional support). Social esteem for intellectuals is a separate factor that does not address the mechanism by which instability normally blocks progress. Athens could have revered its thinkers while still having conditions that should have prevented their success. The paradox requires an explanation of how the usual barriers were circumvented, not merely evidence that intellectuals were popular.
42	   note C: This answer resolves the paradox by identifying the specific mechanism through which political instability normally prevents intellectual advances: the loss of financial support. If instability typically eliminates funding for intellectual work, then the general rule holds because scholars cannot pursue their research without resources. The key phrase is "typically" — Plato and Aristotle were exceptions. Plato came from one of Athens' wealthiest families, and Aristotle was supported by powerful patrons (eventually including Philip of Macedon). Their personal wealth and patronage meant they did not depend on the kind of institutional or governmental funding that political instability would have disrupted. The general rule remains valid: instability normally defunds intellectual work. The counterexample is explained: Plato and Aristotle had independent financial means that insulated them from the usual economic consequences of political turmoil. Both facts coexist perfectly.
43	   note D: The idea that intellectual advances sometimes lead to political stability reverses the direction of the relationship without resolving the paradox. The general rule says stability enables intellectual advances (stability causes progress). This answer says intellectual advances sometimes cause stability (progress causes stability). Even if both directions are sometimes true, this does not explain how Plato and Aristotle achieved their advances during instability. If anything, this answer raises a new puzzle: if intellectual advances can produce stability, why was Athens still unstable despite hosting Plato and Aristotle? The answer addresses a different causal direction from what the paradox requires and leaves the original puzzle untouched.
44	   note E: The fact that other thinkers besides Plato and Aristotle contributed to Athenian intellectual achievement broadens the counterexample rather than resolving it. The paradox is that major intellectual advances occurred during instability. Knowing that more people participated in those advances does not explain how any of them — Plato, Aristotle, or others — managed to succeed despite political turmoil. If anything, this makes the counterexample more robust: it was not just two exceptional individuals defying the odds, but an entire intellectual community thriving during chaos. The paradox gets bigger, not smaller. A resolution needs to explain the mechanism that allowed intellectual work to proceed despite instability, not expand the list of people who accomplished it.
45	 -- explanation style=brief
46	   step0 [The Paradox] Historians find that significant intellectual advances generally require political stability. Yet Plato and Aristotle achieved unparalleled intellectual progress during politically and socially unstable ancient Athens.
47	   step1 [Goal] Find an answer that explains how Plato and Aristotle could have succeeded despite instability — reconciling the counterexample with the general rule rather than eliminating either one.
48	   note A: Changes in political systems since ancient Greece do not explain how Athens produced intellectual advances during instability. This is irrelevant historical context that does not address the paradox.
49	   note B: Social esteem for intellectuals does not explain how instability's usual barriers to intellectual progress were overcome. Being respected does not neutralize the specific obstacles that political turmoil creates.
50	   note C: If instability normally blocks intellectual work by cutting off financial support, Plato and Aristotle are explained as exceptions who had independent wealth. The general rule holds (instability defunds scholarship), and the counterexample is resolved (they had personal resources).
51	   note D: Intellectual advances sometimes causing stability reverses the causal direction without explaining the paradox. The question is how advances happen despite instability, not whether advances produce stability.
52	   note E: More thinkers contributing to Athenian achievements expands the counterexample rather than resolving it. The question is how intellectual progress occurred during instability, not how many people were involved.
53	 -- explanation style=another_way
54	   step0 [The Paradox] Historians say intellectual breakthroughs happen when things are politically calm. Then they turn around and acknowledge that Plato and Aristotle — arguably the greatest intellectual duo in human history — did their best work while Athens was in political chaos. That is like a nutritionist saying candy is terrible for you while admitting that the healthiest person they have ever met eats nothing but Skittles.
55	   step1 [Goal] Find the answer that explains how Plato and Aristotle pulled off their intellectual achievements despite the political turmoil — without destroying the general rule or denying the facts of history.
56	   note A: "Political systems have changed over time." Thank you, every history textbook ever written. But how does that explain why Plato and Aristotle thrived in chaos? It does not. Saying that politics is different now is like explaining why someone survived a plane crash by pointing out that planes have changed since the Wright Brothers. True? Sure. Helpful? Not remotely.
57	   note B: Athens thought smart people were cool. Great. But giving thinkers a standing ovation does not explain how they managed to think straight while the government was falling apart. The question is about the mechanism of how instability blocks intellectual work — and mere popularity does not remove any of those barriers. Imagine a scientist trying to do research during a civil war. "But everyone loves scientists!" they cry, as the lab gets burned down. Esteem is nice, but it does not fix the underlying problem.
58	   note C: The general rule says instability kills intellectual progress. This answer reveals the murder weapon: money. Political chaos typically drains the funding that scholars need to, well, scholar. But Plato and Aristotle were not exactly applying for government grants. Plato was born into one of Athens' wealthiest families, and Aristotle had a Macedonian king bankrolling his research. They were the ancient equivalent of independently wealthy hobbyists who could keep working even when the economy tanked. So the rule still works — instability kills funding, and no funding means no intellectual progress. Plato and Aristotle just had their own money, making them bulletproof to the usual financial consequences of political turmoil. Mystery solved.
59	   note D: This answer is running the argument in reverse — like saying "eating dinner sometimes causes hunger" when someone asked why you can be hungry before dinner. The paradox asks: how can intellectual progress happen without stability? This answer says: intellectual progress sometimes creates stability. Those are two completely different questions. And it raises a new problem: if smart people stabilize politics, why was Athens still a mess with Plato and Aristotle around? This answer makes things more confusing, not less.
60	   note E: "Actually, there were even MORE geniuses thriving during the chaos!" Great — so the counterexample is even bigger than we thought. Instead of explaining how two people defied the rule, now there is a whole crowd of overachievers to account for. This answer is like a detective trying to solve one mysterious disappearance and discovering that actually twenty people vanished. The mystery deepens, not resolves. The paradox needed an explanation, and this answer handed it a megaphone instead.

82	=== PT158-S2-Q18 type=Flaw key=E
83	STIM: Records reveal that of physical therapy patients who received less than six weeks of treatment, about 31 percent showed major improvement, regardless of whether they were treated by a general practitioner or by a specialist. Of patients who received physical therapy for a longer time, again regardless of whether they were treated by a general practitioner or by a specialist, about 50 percent showed major improvement. Therefore, the choice between seeing a specialist or a general practitioner for necessary physical therapy will not affect one's chances of major improvement.
84	STEM: The reasoning in the argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument
85	  (A) [Not Assumed] presumes, without providing justification, that effectiveness of different practitioners in bringing about major improvement cannot differ at all if their effectiveness in bringing about any improvement does not differ
86	  (B) [Not an Objection] provides no information about the kinds of injuries that require short-term as opposed to long-term treatment
87	  (C) [Not an Objection] overlooks the possibility that patients are more strongly biased to report favorably on one of the two types of medical professionals than on the other
88	  (D) [Not Assumed] fails to indicate whether the number of patients surveyed who saw a general practitioner was equal to the number who saw a specialist
89	  (E) [Correct] overlooks the possibility that specialists and general practitioners each tend to excel at treating a different type of injury
90	 -- explanation style=standard
91	   step0 [Conclusion (therefore)] The choice between seeing a specialist or a general practitioner for needed PT will not affect one's chances of major improvement.
92	   step1 [Evidence] Among people who received less than six weeks of PT, 31% showed major improvement, whether they were treated by a general practitioner or a by a specialist. Among people who received more PT, again about 50% showed major improvement, regardless of whether they were treated by a general practitioner or by a specialist.
93	   step2 [Evaluate] This conclusion is essentially anti-causal, saying it doesn't make a difference whether you're assigned to a general practitioner or to a specialist. If we were going to argue that it does make a difference which one you're assigned to, then how are we going to explain why people seem to be getting the same rate of major improvement regardless of which type of PT they see? There is a tiny detail that crops up in the conclusion about "necessary physical therapy". The evidence didn't say that the people receiving physical therapy were getting needed physical therapy. Maybe when it's needed, it really makes a difference whether you see a GP vs. a specialist, but the records are distorted by the fact that tons of people get PT when they don't need it. Maybe we're currently doing a great job of assigning people to a GP vs. a specialist based on what their specific situation is, and if we stopped caring about which type of therapist we assigned patients, the 31% and 50% marks would go way down. Conversely, maybe we're currently not doing a great job of assigning people to a GP vs. a specialist based on their specific situation, and if we started caring about which type we assigned patients to, we would do better than 31% and 50% of patients showing major improvement.
94	   step3 [Goal] The author is assuming that if both types of therapist are getting the same percent of their patients to show major improvement, then it doesn't matter whether a patient sees one or the other (for the sake of major improvement). But the author is failing to consider that those current rates of major improvement might change were we to concentrate more or concentrate less on caring about whether patients saw a specialist vs. GP based on their specific needs.
95	   note A: The argument does not presume that effectiveness should be measured solely by improvement rates. In fact, improvement rate is a reasonable metric for evaluating treatment effectiveness. The argument's flaw is not the choice of metric but the failure to control for confounding variables within that metric. Even if we accept improvement rate as the right measure, the comparison is still flawed because it does not account for differences in the types of conditions treated by specialists versus generalists. This answer misidentifies the problem: the measurement itself is fine; the comparison methodology is what fails. Two practitioners can both be measured by improvement rate, and the comparison can still be meaningless if they are treating fundamentally different patient populations.
96	   note B: Knowing which kinds of injuries require short-term versus long-term treatment is unnecessary for the argument's conclusion. The argument groups patients by duration and compares outcomes within each group. Whether back injuries need six weeks and knee injuries need twelve is irrelevant to the question of whether practitioner type affects outcomes. The argument's flaw is about who treats what types of patients, not about which injuries require which treatment durations. This answer points to missing information that would be useful for a different analysis but does not identify the logical error in the argument as presented.
97	   note C: Patient reporting bias — whether patients are more favorable toward one type of practitioner — is a valid methodological concern in general, but it is not the specific flaw in this argument. The argument assumes the reported improvement rates are accurate and draws a flawed conclusion from them. Even if the data is perfectly unbiased, the comparison fails because it does not control for differences in the patient populations seen by each type of practitioner. Introducing a reporting bias concern shifts attention from the argument's actual logical error (comparing non-equivalent groups) to a hypothetical data quality issue that, even if resolved, would not fix the underlying problem.
98	   note D: Whether the number of patients surveyed was equal between groups is not the argument's flaw. Statistical comparisons of percentages do not require equal sample sizes — the percentages are already normalized. The argument compares 31% to 31% and 50% to 50%; these percentages can be equally meaningful whether derived from 100 patients or 10,000. The flaw is that equal percentages from different patient populations are being treated as evidence of equal effectiveness. If specialists treat harder cases, equal improvement rates actually indicate superior performance by specialists. The sample size question is a statistical concern that is separate from and less fundamental than the confounding-variable problem that actually undermines the argument.
99	   note E: This answer identifies the argument's central flaw: it compares raw improvement rates without accounting for the possibility that specialists and generalists treat fundamentally different types of conditions. If specialists tend to handle complex, difficult cases (torn ligaments, post-surgical rehabilitation, chronic conditions) while generalists handle simpler problems (mild strains, basic mobility issues), then equal improvement rates actually indicate that specialists are more effective — they are achieving the same results with harder cases. Conversely, if generalists excel at treating certain conditions and specialists at others, a patient's choice of practitioner absolutely matters, even though the aggregate numbers look identical. The argument committed the ecological fallacy: drawing individual-level conclusions from group-level data that masks crucial subgroup differences. By overlooking that each practitioner type might have a comparative advantage with different conditions, the argument prematurely concluded that the choice does not matter.
100	 -- explanation style=brief
101	   step0 [Conclusion] The choice between a specialist and a general practitioner for physical therapy will not affect one's chances of major improvement.
102	   step1 [Evidence] Patients treated for less than six weeks showed 31% improvement regardless of practitioner type. Patients treated for longer showed 50% improvement regardless of practitioner type.
103	   step2 [Evaluate] The data groups patients by treatment duration and shows equal rates within each group. But this ignores the possibility that specialists and generalists treat different types of patients. If specialists handle harder cases and generalists handle easier ones, equal raw percentages could mask a real advantage for one type of practitioner.
104	   step3 [Goal] Find the flaw: the argument overlooks that the types of patients each practitioner sees might differ, making the raw comparison misleading.
105	   note A: Improvement rate is a reasonable metric. The flaw is not the measurement but the failure to control for different patient populations, making the comparison misleading despite using a valid metric.
106	   note B: Which injuries require short versus long treatment is irrelevant. The flaw concerns whether specialists and generalists treat different conditions, not the duration-injury relationship.
107	   note C: Reporting bias is a data quality concern, not the argument's flaw. Even with perfectly accurate data, the comparison is still flawed because it does not control for different patient populations.
108	   note D: Equal sample sizes are not needed for comparing percentages. The flaw is that equal percentages from different patient populations do not prove equal effectiveness — a confounding variable issue, not a sample size issue.
109	   note E: If specialists treat harder cases and generalists treat easier ones, matching improvement rates actually show specialists are more effective. The argument ignored that different practitioner types may excel at different conditions, making the aggregate comparison misleading.
110	 -- explanation style=another_way
111	   step0 [Conclusion] Does not matter whether you see a specialist or a general practitioner for physical therapy — your odds are the same either way.
112	   step1 [Evidence] The numbers look identical: 31% improvement for short-term patients with either type of practitioner, 50% for long-term patients with either type. Case closed? Not quite.
113	   step2 [Evaluate] Imagine a heart surgeon and a family doctor both have a 90% patient survival rate. Sounds equal, right? But the heart surgeon is doing open-heart surgery while the family doctor is treating colds. Equal percentages mean nothing if the difficulty of the cases is completely different. The argument looked at matching numbers and declared a draw without asking: are these practitioners even treating the same kinds of patients? If specialists handle the tough cases and generalists handle the easy ones, the "equal" outcomes actually prove specialists are better, not that the choice does not matter.
114	   step3 [Goal] Spot the answer that calls out this apples-to-oranges comparison — the possibility that different practitioners treat different types of conditions.
115	   note A: There is nothing wrong with using improvement rates to measure how well a treatment works — that is actually a pretty sensible approach. The problem is not the ruler; it is that the argument measured two completely different things and declared them equal. Imagine weighing a bowling ball and a watermelon and, finding they weigh the same, declaring them identical objects. The scale is fine. The comparison is the problem.
116	   note B: Sure, it would be interesting to know which injuries need a quick fix versus a long haul. But that is not the argument's problem. The argument's problem is that it compared two practitioners without checking if they were treating the same kinds of patients. Knowing the duration-injury connection is like asking what color the walls are when the building's foundation is cracked — interesting detail, wrong problem.
117	   note C: Are patients fudging their reviews? Maybe, but that is a data accuracy problem, not the argument's logical problem. The argument could have the most honest, most accurate data on the planet and still be wrong, because it compared practitioners who might be treating completely different types of patients. Fixing the Yelp reviews does not fix the fundamental apples-to-oranges comparison. This answer is polishing the telescope while pointing it at the wrong star.
118	   note D: The "but was the sample size the same?" objection misses the forest for the trees. Percentages are already built to handle different group sizes — that is literally what percentages are for. The problem is not how many patients were counted but what kinds of patients were counted. If specialists treat broken spines while generalists treat sprained ankles, getting the same improvement percentage is not evidence of equal skill. It is evidence that the specialist is performing miracles while the generalist is handling routine cases. The sizes of the groups are a footnote; the composition of the groups is the headline.
119	   note E: Picture this: a brain surgeon and a dermatologist both have a 95% success rate. Equal, right? The brain surgeon is removing tumors and the dermatologist is treating acne. Those identical numbers tell a very different story once you know what each doctor is actually treating. The argument did exactly this — it saw matching percentages for specialists and generalists and declared them equally effective without asking the obvious question: are they treating the same kinds of patients? If the specialist is rehabilitating complex post-surgical cases while the generalist is stretching out someone with a stiff neck, matching numbers mean the specialist is crushing it. The argument looked at the scoreboard without checking who was playing which opponent.

141	=== PT158-S3-Q20 type=Must Be True key=B
142	STIM: The study of primates is interesting for many reasons, including the fact that only primates have opposable thumbs. The lemurs are lower primates and the only primates indigenous to Madagascar, a large island off the coast of southeastern Africa. Some species of lemurs are the only living lower primates that are diurnal—that is, active primarily during the day. All higher primates are thought to have evolved from a single diurnal species of lower primates.
143	STEM: Which one of the following can be properly inferred from the information above?
144	  (A) [Supported But Not Certain] The chimpanzee, a higher primate, evolved from the lemur.
145	  (B) [Correct] No primates indigenous to Madagascar are diurnal higher primates.
146	  (C) [Too Strong] No higher primate is nocturnal.
147	  (D) [No Support] There are some lemurs without opposable thumbs.
148	  (E) [Too Strong] There are no nocturnal lemurs.
149	 -- explanation style=standard
150	   step0 [Information] ```
151	conditionals (only / the only / all)
152	have opposable thumbs → primate
153	primate indigenous → lemurs
154	to Madagascar
155	lower primates and → certain lemurs
156	diurnal and living
157	higher primate → evolved from one single
158	diurnal lower primate
159	```
160	   step1 [Evaluate] We're trying to figure out whether we can combine any of these claims. We got multiple conditionals, but it doesn't seem like any of them chain together. What are the Overlapping Concepts? Lots. Primates, lower primates, lemurs, and diurnal all show up multiple times. Given that we were told that all higher primates evolved from a single diurnal species of lower primates, and given that some species of lemur are the only living lower primates that are diurnal, we have some support for the inference that all higher primates evolved from a lemur species, but this is Must Be True, not Most Supported. We don't know that for sure.
161	   step2 [Goal] Look for a provable answer. It's hard to predict what it will be since this assortment of facts didn't lend itself to any obvious combining / synthesizing.
162	   note A: The passage states that all higher primates evolved from a single diurnal lower primate species, but it never identifies that species as a lemur. The passage says some lemurs are diurnal lower primates, and higher primates evolved from a diurnal lower primate — but these could be different species of diurnal lower primates. The passage does not establish that the ancestral species was a lemur, let alone a chimpanzee's specific ancestor. Furthermore, "evolved from" describes a distant ancestral relationship, not a direct lineage claim linking chimpanzees specifically to lemurs. This inference requires assumptions beyond what the passage states.
163	   note B: The passage states that lemurs are the only primates indigenous to Madagascar (F3) and that lemurs are lower primates (F2). From these two facts, it follows necessarily that all primates indigenous to Madagascar are lower primates — and therefore no primates indigenous to Madagascar are higher primates. Since no higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar, no diurnal higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar either. This answer captures exactly this inference: "no primates indigenous to Madagascar are diurnal higher primates." The reasoning is airtight — if the entire set (higher primates) is excluded from Madagascar, then any subset of that set (diurnal higher primates) is also excluded. No additional assumptions are needed.
164	   note C: The passage says all higher primates are thought to have evolved from a single diurnal species of lower primates. This tells us the ancestral species was diurnal, but it does not tell us that all current higher primates are diurnal. Evolution from a diurnal ancestor does not guarantee that all descendants remain diurnal. Many higher primate species could have evolved nocturnal habits over millions of years. In fact, some higher primates (like certain prosimians reclassified or owl monkeys) are nocturnal. The passage provides no information about the current activity patterns of higher primates as a group — only about their evolutionary origin.
165	   note D: The passage states that only primates have opposable thumbs — meaning ALL primates have them, and nothing else does. This means lemurs, as primates, have opposable thumbs. The passage does not say or imply that any lemurs lack opposable thumbs. The "only primates" formulation establishes that opposable thumbs are exclusive to primates, but it does not address variation within the primate order. Since lemurs are primates, the passage actually implies they DO have opposable thumbs, making this answer contradict the passage rather than follow from it.
166	   note E: The passage says some species of lemurs are the only living lower primates that are diurnal. This means some lemur species are diurnal, but it does not say all lemurs are diurnal. The word "some" leaves open the possibility that other lemur species are nocturnal. In fact, many lemur species in the real world are nocturnal, and the passage's careful use of "some species of lemurs" rather than "all lemurs" signals that not every lemur is diurnal. This answer claims there are no nocturnal lemurs, which goes beyond — and likely contradicts — the passage's careful "some" qualifier.
167	 -- explanation style=brief
168	   step0 [Statements] Lemurs are the only primates indigenous to Madagascar. Lemurs are lower primates. Some lemur species are the only diurnal lower primates. All higher primates evolved from a single diurnal lower primate species.
169	   step1 [Evaluate] Since lemurs are the ONLY primates in Madagascar, and lemurs are lower primates, no higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar. Any primates in Madagascar — diurnal or not — are lower primates (lemurs).
170	   step2 [Goal] Find the inference that follows necessarily. The key deduction: no primates indigenous to Madagascar are diurnal higher primates, because no primates indigenous to Madagascar are higher primates at all.
171	   note A: The passage never identifies the ancestral diurnal lower primate as a lemur. Higher primates evolved from "a single diurnal species of lower primates," but that species may not be a lemur.
172	   note B: Lemurs are the only primates in Madagascar, and lemurs are lower primates. So no higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar — and therefore no diurnal higher primates are either. This follows necessarily from the stated facts.
173	   note C: Evolving from a diurnal ancestor does not guarantee all descendants are diurnal. The passage tells us about the ancestral species, not about the current activity patterns of all higher primates.
174	   note D: The passage says only primates have opposable thumbs. Since lemurs are primates, they have them. Nothing suggests any lemurs lack opposable thumbs.
175	   note E: The passage says "some species of lemurs" are diurnal, not all. "Some" leaves room for other lemur species to be nocturnal. This answer eliminates nocturnal lemurs entirely, contradicting the passage's careful qualifier.
176	 -- explanation style=another_way
177	   step0 [Statements] Lemurs are Madagascar's only primates, and lemurs are lower primates. Some lemurs are diurnal (day-active). Higher primates all evolved from some diurnal lower primate species. And only primates have opposable thumbs — a fun fact that mostly sits on the sidelines here.
178	   step1 [Evaluate] The big inference: Madagascar has lemurs and only lemurs when it comes to primates. Lemurs are lower primates. So: zero higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar. That means no diurnal higher primates in Madagascar either — because there are no higher primates there, period. It is a simple subset argument: if no X exists in location Y, then no special type of X exists in location Y either.
179	   step2 [Goal] Find the answer that captures: no primates in Madagascar are higher primates (and therefore none are diurnal higher primates).
180	   note A: The passage says higher primates evolved from a diurnal lower primate species. It also says some lemurs are diurnal lower primates. But it NEVER says the ancestral species was a lemur. Maybe it was a completely different, long-extinct diurnal lower primate that is not a lemur at all. The passage carefully avoids connecting these dots, and this answer tries to force the connection. Close, tempting, but a bridge too far for a "must be true" question.
181	   note B: Madagascar has lemurs and only lemurs when it comes to primates. Lemurs are lower primates. So there are zero higher primates in Madagascar — diurnal, nocturnal, or otherwise. You cannot find a diurnal higher primate in Madagascar because you cannot find ANY higher primate in Madagascar. It is like asking "are there any red sports cars in the parking lot?" when the parking lot is empty. No cars, no red sports cars. No higher primates, no diurnal higher primates. Simple, clean, and guaranteed by the facts.
182	   note C: Just because great-great-great-grandpa was a morning person does not mean every descendant wakes up at dawn. The passage says higher primates evolved from a diurnal species, not that they all stayed diurnal. Evolution is not a photocopier — traits change over millions of years. Some higher primates might have gone nocturnal along the way. The passage tells us where they came from, not where they all ended up. This answer treats ancestry as destiny, which is not how evolution works.
183	   note D: The passage says "only primates have opposable thumbs" — which means if you are a primate, you have them. Lemurs are primates. Therefore, lemurs have opposable thumbs. This answer tries to claim some lemurs are missing them, which goes against the grain of everything the passage said. It is like being told "only birds have feathers" and concluding "some birds do not have feathers." The passage gave lemurs their opposable thumbs by including them in the primate club. This answer tries to take those thumbs away without any textual support.
184	   note E: The passage said "SOME species of lemurs" are diurnal. SOME. Not all. Not every single one. Some. That little word is doing a lot of work, and this answer steamrolls right over it. If every lemur were diurnal, the passage would not have bothered with "some." The fact that it specified "some species" strongly implies other lemur species are NOT diurnal — meaning nocturnal lemurs very likely exist. This answer says they do not, which contradicts the passage's own careful word choice.

233	STEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the argument above?
234	  (A) [Negation] The argument presumes, without providing justification, that if a person’s promise is not selfishly motivated then that promise is reliable.
235	  (B) [Too Strong] The argument presumes, without providing justification, that promises made for selfish reasons are never kept.
236	  (C) [Wrong Flaw] The argument confuses the effect of an action with its cause.
237	  (D) [Correct] The argument overlooks the fact that a promise need not be unreliable just because the person who made it had an ulterior motive for doing so.
238	  (E) [Wrong Conclusion] The argument overlooks the fact that a candidate who makes promises for selfish reasons may nonetheless be worthy of the office for which he or she is running.
239	 -- explanation style=single

241	But one must not forget that the politicians’ purpose in giving these speeches is to get themselves elected.
242	Conclusion

244	Evaluate
245	This is an ad hominem attack. Just because politicians are motivated to win reelection does not mean that their promises are unreliable.
246	   note A: This negates the assumption of the argument that if a person's promise is selfishly motivated then that promise is unreliable.
247	   note B: The argument does not go so far as to say that such promises are never kept, but rather says that they are unreliable.
248	   note C: - paragraph
249	
250	- paragraph
251	
252	This correctly describes a flaw that is not contained in the argument. This describes a causal reasoning error.
253	   note D: This correctly describes the difference between a questionable source and an unreliable claim.

364	=== PT147-S4-Q4 type=Sufficient Assumption key=A
365	STIM: Economist: Unemployment will soon decrease. If total government spending significantly increases next year, the economy will be stimulated in the short term and unemployment will decrease. If, on the other hand, total government spending significantly decreases next year, businesses will retain more of their earnings in the short term and employ more workers, thereby decreasing unemployment.
366	STEM: The conclusion drawn by the economist is properly inferred if which one of the following is assumed?
367	  (A) [Correct] Either total government spending will significantly decrease next year or else total government spending will significantly increase next year.
368	  (B) [Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending] Government officials are currently implementing policies that are intended to reduce unemployment.
369	  (C) [Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending] If there is a significantly increased demand for workers, then there will be a significant decrease in unemployment.
370	  (D) [Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending] A significant increase in total government spending will slow the economy in the long run.
371	  (E) [Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending] If the economy is not stimulated and businesses do not retain more of their earnings, then unemployment will not decrease.
372	 -- explanation style=single
373	   step0 [Conclusion] Unemployment will soon decrease.
374	   step1 [Evidence] If total govt spending significantly goes up, unemployment will decrease.
375	
376	If total govt spending significantly decreases, unemployment will decrease.
377	   step2 [Evaluate] We ascertain that the first claim is the conclusion by seeing that the other two claims are clearly meant to work together. Conjunctions/connections like "and / but / on the other hand" show us that two claims are interacting together to move the argument forward.
378	
379	Each of the conditional premises involves TWO consequences, one of which is decreasing unemployment. I didn't bother writing out the other half of each outcome, because all we care about is proving that there will be decreasing unemployment.
380	
381	At first, this argument seems pretty reasonable. It seems like an *Either Way You Slice It* style of argument: "Whether spending increases significantly or decreases significantly, we end up with decreasing unemployment. So either way you slice it, we're gonna have decreasing unemployment."
382	
383	But we know that an argument on Sufficient Assumption can't *already* be airtight. There has to be some gap, so that the correct answer can make it airtight.
384	
385	How would unemployment *fail to decrease*?
386	
387	Well, I guess spending doesn't necessarily have to go significantly up or significantly down. It could more or less stay the same.
388	
389	The author is presenting a *False Choice* (a false binary / a false dichotomy), acting like spending will either go up a bunch or down a bunch.
390	
391	But she's failing to consider the middle ground, where spending basically stays the same.
392	   step3 [Goal] We just need to rule out the possibility that govt spending will stay basically the same. We need to know that it'll definitely go up or down significantly.
393	   note A: The economist covers two cases: spending goes up significantly, or spending goes down significantly. Both lead to lower unemployment. But nothing says one of those two things will happen. Spending could stay about the same, and then neither rule applies.
394	
395	This answer closes that gap. If spending will either rise or fall significantly, one of the two cases must happen, and either way unemployment decreases. The conclusion follows.
396	   note B: Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
397	   note C: Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will gone up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
398	   note D: Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
399	   note E: Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
400

409	=== PT156-S2-Q19 type=Weaken key=B
410	STIM: Geologist: As a spinning object's radius decreases, its rate of rotation increases; for example, figure skaters can twirl faster by bringing their arms closer to their sides. This law of physics helps to explain why, over the past decade, Earth's rate of rotation increased. For earthquakes have increased in number and severity during the past decade; and since immediately after an earthquake Earth's tectonic plates move toward the center of the earth, their movement causes Earth's radius to decrease.
411	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, would most undermine the geologist's explanation of why Earth's rate of rotation increased over the last decade?
412	  (A) [Opposite (if anything): more correlation strenthens, if anything] In the beginning of the twentieth century, geologists observed an increase in number and severity of earthquakes, accompanied by a decrease of Earth's radius.
413	  (B) [Correct] During the past decade, other geological events have counteracted the movements of the tectonic plates that occur immediately after earthquakes.
414	  (C) [No Impact] Only skillful figure skaters succeed in twirling faster by bringing their arms closer to their sides.
415	  (D) [No Impact] Since the time of the ancient Egyptians, Earth's rate of rotation has been known to fluctuate.
416	  (E) [No Impact] Increased volcanic activity over the last ten years suggests that the overall movement of the tectonic plates has increased.
417	 -- explanation style=single
418	   step0 [Conclusion] Earth's rotation sped up over the past decade because more earthquakes shrank Earth's radius.
419	   step1 [Evidence] A spinning object speeds up as its radius shrinks. Earthquakes increased in number and severity. Right after an earthquake, tectonic plates move toward Earth's center, shrinking the radius.
420	   step2 [Evaluate] The geologist assumes the plate movement after earthquakes produced a net decrease in the radius. If other forces pushed the other way, the radius may not have shrunk at all, and the explanation falls apart.
421	   step3 [Goal] Show the radius may not have shrunk overall.
422	   note A: Another case of more earthquakes going with a smaller radius supports the geologist, if anything.
423	   note B: Disrupts the assumption that the tectonic plate movement causes a net decrease in the Earth's radius by raising the possiblitiy that any decreases are counteracted by other movements.
424	   note C: Limiting the example to a subset of skaters doesn't change the law of physics the example is exemplifying.
425	   note D: This might seem at first like an alternative explanation for the current rate of rotation increase. It isn't, however, because the reason for the historic fluctuations aren't specified. We can't, therefore, say the current change in rotation rate is any less likely to be due to the contraction of Earth's radius that it was before we knew that similar rotation fluctuations have happened throughout history.
426	   note E: The overall amount of tectonic movement increasing in the last decade doesn't attack the idea that the movement ultimately leads to a net decrease in the size of the Earth.
427
