1	=== PT158-S3-Q9 type=Weaken key=A
2	STIM: Consumer advocate: Even relatively minor drug-related interactions can still be harmful to patients. For example, aspirin taken with fruit juice is ineffective. People unaware of this suffer unnecessary discomfort or take more aspirin than necessary. The government should, therefore, require drug companies to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions.
3	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the consumer advocate's argument?
4	  (A) [Correct] Providing information on minor drug-related interactions would detract from a patient's attention to serious interactions.
5	  (B) [Too Weak] Many drugs have fewer documented drug-related interactions than does aspirin.
6	  (C) [Too Weak] Providing information about all drug-related interactions would result in only negligible price increases for consumers.
7	  (D) [No Impact] Current research is such that many drug-related interactions have not yet been identified.
8	  (E) [Strengthens, If Anything] Pharmacists usually draw patients' attention to printed warnings that are provided with drugs.
9	 -- explanation style=standard
10	   step0 [Conclusion (Therefore)] The government should require drug companies to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions.
11	   step1 [Evidence] Relatively minor drug-related interactions can still be harmful to patients (such as the fact that fruit juice can render aspirin ineffective, leading to people either not getting the pain relief they expected or to people taking more aspirin than necessary).
12	   step2 [Evaluate] Given that ... relatively minor drug-related interactions can be harmful to patients (such as the fruit juice / aspirin example), How can we argue that ... drug companies should not be required to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions? The author is presenting one potential upside of this Plan / Recommendation (people taking aspirin would realize not to take it with fruit juice). We should try to think of potential downsides.
13	   step3 [Goal] Look for an answer bringing up something negative about the plan of requiring drug companies to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions. Is there some way in which it wouldn't be feasible or cost prohibitive? (Maybe it's impossible or impossibly costly to figure out every single way a new drug could interact with every other substance. Could it backfire in some way? (would people suddenly be afraid to take their needed medicines because they read about a possible interaction that is extremely rare but scares them out of taking their meds?)
14	   note A: This answer identifies the self-defeating consequence of the proposed policy. The consumer advocate wants all drug interactions disclosed so patients can avoid harmful interactions. But if the volume of information about minor interactions overwhelms patients, they will pay less attention to the serious, potentially life-threatening interactions that matter most. The policy would achieve the opposite of its intended purpose: instead of making patients safer, it would make them less safe by diluting their attention across a massive list of interactions, most of which are minor. The aspirin-and-fruit-juice type interactions would crowd out warnings about interactions that could cause organ damage, severe allergic reactions, or death. This is a classic "more is not always better" weakener — the proposed remedy creates a new problem that is worse than the original one.
15	   note B: The fact that many drugs have fewer interactions than aspirin does not undermine the consumer advocate's argument. The advocate's proposal covers all drugs, not just aspirin. Even if most drugs have fewer interactions, the policy still requires disclosure of all known interactions for every drug. A drug with five interactions instead of fifty still requires those five to be disclosed under the proposed policy. The volume of information per drug is not the issue — the advocate's argument is about the principle of comprehensive disclosure regardless of quantity. This answer provides context about the aspirin example but does not challenge the reasoning or the policy recommendation in any meaningful way.
16	   note C: If providing information about all interactions would result in only negligible additional costs to drug companies, this removes a potential practical objection to the policy, but it does not address the argument's logical vulnerability. The consumer advocate's argument could be weakened by showing the policy would be ineffective or counterproductive, not by showing it would be inexpensive. The cost of implementation is a practical concern, and showing that costs are negligible actually makes the policy look more feasible, not less desirable. This answer addresses a potential objection (cost) that the argument never raised, and it addresses it in a way that supports the policy rather than undermining it. The real weakness is about information overload, not financial burden.
17	   note D: The fact that many interactions have not yet been identified is irrelevant to whether the government should require disclosure of those that have been identified. The advocate's proposal covers "all known" interactions, and the existence of unknown ones does not affect the case for disclosing known ones. If anything, this answer might slightly strengthen the advocate's position by suggesting the problem is even larger than currently understood — but that would only affect the scope of future disclosure, not the current proposal. The argument is about whether known interactions should be disclosed, not about the completeness of current research.
18	   note E: If pharmacists draw patients' attention to printed warnings, this supports the idea that disclosure actually works — people do read and act on the information they receive. The consumer advocate argues that patients should be given interaction information, and this answer confirms that when warnings are provided, healthcare professionals help ensure patients notice them. Rather than weakening the argument, this answer provides evidence that the disclosure mechanism is effective: warnings are not ignored but are actively communicated by pharmacists. This makes the case for comprehensive disclosure stronger, since it suggests the information would actually reach and influence patients.
19	 -- explanation style=brief
20	   step0 [Conclusion] The government should require drug companies to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions.
21	   step1 [Evidence] Even minor drug interactions can be harmful. For example, aspirin taken with fruit juice is ineffective, causing people to suffer needlessly or take excessive aspirin.
22	   step2 [Evaluate] The argument assumes that providing all interaction information would be beneficial on balance. If the sheer volume of information overwhelmed patients, they might miss the most important warnings while being distracted by trivial ones.
23	   step3 [Goal] Find an answer showing that the proposed notification requirement would cause more harm than good — likely by creating information overload that undermines attention to serious interactions.
24	   note A: Information overload from minor interactions would cause patients to overlook serious ones. The policy would backfire: instead of improving safety, it would reduce attention to the interactions that matter most.
25	   note B: Fewer interactions per drug does not undermine the case for disclosure. The advocate's argument applies to all drugs and all interactions regardless of how many each drug has.
26	   note C: Low costs make the policy more feasible, not less desirable. The argument's weakness is about whether the policy would be effective, not whether it would be expensive. This answer supports the proposal rather than undermining it.
27	   note D: Unknown interactions do not affect the case for disclosing known ones. The advocate proposes disclosing "all known" interactions; the existence of undiscovered ones is beside the point.
28	   note E: Pharmacists helping patients notice warnings confirms that the disclosure system works. This supports rather than undermines the case for requiring more comprehensive warnings.
29	 -- explanation style=another_way
30	   step0 [Conclusion] Drug companies should be forced to warn consumers about every known drug interaction. All of them.
31	   step1 [Evidence] Even small interactions matter. Case in point: aspirin plus fruit juice equals useless aspirin. People who do not know this suffer unnecessarily or pop extra aspirin. So all interactions should be disclosed.
32	   step2 [Evaluate] The heart is in the right place, but "tell people everything" is not always the best information strategy. Have you ever read the full side effects list on a medication? It includes everything from "mild headache" to "spontaneous combustion." When everything is flagged as important, nothing is. The advocate assumes more information always helps, but drowning patients in warnings about every minor interaction could cause them to tune out the warnings that actually matter.
33	   step3 [Goal] Find the answer showing that this well-intentioned policy would backfire — that listing every interaction would make patients pay less attention to the dangerous ones.
34	   note A: The classic "boy who cried wolf" problem, applied to drug labels. List every single interaction — including "this pill is slightly less effective if you eat a grapefruit" — and patients will stop reading before they get to "this pill combined with that pill will send you to the emergency room." The advocate wanted to help people by giving them more information, but this answer shows that more information would actually make people less safe. When everything is highlighted as important, nothing stands out. The minor interactions would drown out the major ones, and the patients who most need to pay attention would have already tuned out ten warnings ago.
35	   note B: "Most drugs have fewer interactions than aspirin." Okay, so the aspirin example was dramatic. That does not help. The advocate is not arguing "all drugs are as interaction-heavy as aspirin." The advocate is arguing that any unknown interaction can cause harm, so all interactions should be disclosed. Whether that means listing fifty warnings or five per drug does not change the principle. This answer quibbles with the illustration while the main argument stands untouched.
36	   note C: "It would not even cost that much!" is an argument FOR the policy, not against it. The question asks what weakens the advocate's position, and cheap implementation makes the proposal more attractive, not less. The real problem is not whether drug companies can afford to print more warnings — it is whether patients will actually benefit from reading them. A free policy that makes patients less safe is still a bad policy. This answer is cheering for the wrong team.
37	   note D: "There are interactions we have not found yet." Okay, so the iceberg is bigger than we thought. But the advocate is talking about disclosing the part we can see — the "known" interactions. Undiscovered interactions are a problem for researchers, not for this particular policy debate. It is like telling someone who wants to ban a list of toxic chemicals that there are probably more toxic chemicals out there. That does not make banning the known ones a worse idea; it just means there is more work to do later.
38	   note E: Pharmacists are actually reading the warnings to patients? That is great news — for the consumer advocate. It means the disclosure system is working: print the warnings, pharmacists make sure patients hear about them, patients make better decisions. This is an argument FOR more disclosure, not against it. If pharmacists are already effective at communicating existing warnings, they would presumably do the same with new ones. The advocate could cite this answer as evidence that the proposed policy would actually work as intended.
39

55	=== PT158-S3-Q13 type=Necessary Assumption key=D
56	STIM: Psychotherapist: The troubles from which a patient seeks relief through psychotherapy do not have purely internal causes; rather, those troubles result in part from the patient's relationships with other people. Hence, to help the patient heal, the psychotherapist must focus on the need for positive change in those relationships.
57	STEM: Which one of the following is an assumption the psychotherapist's argument requires?
58	  (A) [Out of Scope / Too Strong] Psychotherapists cannot help change their patients' relationships unless those patients focus on other people's troubles.
59	  (B) [Out of Scope] At least some psychotherapy patients cannot be healed if a psychotherapist helps them change their relationships with other people.
60	  (C) [Too Strong] Those psychotherapy patients who change their relationships with other people will thereby find relief from at least some of their troubles.
61	  (D) [Correct] No psychotherapist can help a patient heal solely by addressing the internal causes of the patient's troubles.
62	  (E) [Unrelated to Conclusion] If a psychotherapist helps patients focus on troubles that are purely internal, then that psychotherapist thereby provides them the relief they seek.
63	 -- explanation style=standard
64	   step0 [Conclusion (Hence)] To help a patient heal, the psychotherapist must focus on the need for positive change in the patient's relationships with other people.
65	   step1 [Evidence] The trouble from which a patient seeks to heal do not have purely internal causes. They result in part form the patient's relationships with other people.
66	   step2 [Evaluate] The logic of this argument seems to be that, "Since the causes of the problems are partly relationships with other people, then it's impossible to help alleviate those problems unless you focus on positively changing those relationships." So the author is assuming, "If a problem is caused in part by X, then helping to solve the problem requires addressing X." We could potentially argue that merely helping a patient work on the internal causes (increase their self-esteem / decrease their anxiety) could do enough good that they heal somewhat, even though we never forced them to work on their relationships.
67	   step3 [Goal] Whenever a conclusion takes the form of, "in order to do X, you must do Y", we are assuming two things: Y in fact would help with X; nothing else would otherwise help with X. So let's look for an answer saying "focusing on positive change in those relationships could potentially reduce those relationships' being a cause of a patient's troubles" or "it is not possible to help a problem partly caused by relationships without addressing relationships"
68	   note A: This answer makes two problematic claims. First, it introduces a requirement about patients focusing on "the troubles of other people," which is not what the argument discusses. The argument is about patients' own relationships, not about focusing on other people's troubles. Second, the claim that psychotherapists "cannot help change" patients' relationships without this focus goes far beyond what the argument needs to assume. The argument needs only the assumption that internal-only treatment is insufficient for healing — it does not need any specific claim about what prevents therapists from changing relationships. This answer mischaracterizes the relational focus the argument describes and imposes a requirement the argument does not demand.
69	   note B: This answer says some patients cannot be healed even if a therapist helps them change their relationships. The argument's conclusion is that therapists must focus on relational change; the assumption needed is about why that focus is necessary. Knowing that relational change sometimes fails to produce healing does not help establish that relational focus is necessary — if anything, it suggests that relational focus might not be sufficient, which is a different concern entirely. The argument needs the assumption that internal-only treatment is insufficient, not the claim that relational treatment is sometimes insufficient too. This answer addresses the potential limitations of the proposed approach rather than establishing why the approach is necessary in the first place.
70	   note C: This answer claims that patients who change their relationships will "thereby find relief from their troubles." This is a guarantee — if you change the relationships, healing follows. But the argument does not need this strong a claim. The argument needs only that therapy cannot succeed by addressing internal causes alone — it needs the relational focus to be necessary, not to be sufficient by itself. The argument's conclusion is that therapists must focus on relational change, not that relational change will definitely cure patients. Negate this answer: "some patients who change their relationships will not find relief." The argument survives this negation perfectly well — even if relational change is not guaranteed to work, it could still be a necessary component of therapy. The argument does not promise success; it identifies a necessary focus area.
71	   note D: This answer states that no psychotherapist can help a patient heal solely by addressing internal causes. This is exactly the assumption that bridges the gap between the evidence (troubles are partly relational) and the conclusion (therapy must focus on relationships). If a therapist COULD heal a patient by addressing only internal causes, then the relational focus would not be necessary — it might be helpful but not required. But if internal-only treatment cannot achieve healing, then the relational component becomes essential, and the therapist "must" focus on it. Apply the negation test: "some psychotherapist CAN help a patient heal solely by addressing internal causes." If this were true, the conclusion that therapists must focus on relationships would collapse — internal-only treatment could suffice, making relational focus optional rather than mandatory. The negation destroys the argument, confirming this is a necessary assumption.
72	   note E: This answer discusses what happens when a therapist focuses on "purely internal" troubles, but the argument's conclusion is about what the therapist must focus on (relationships), not about the consequences of focusing on the wrong thing. Additionally, the claim that focusing on internal troubles "will not help patients address any difficulties they have in their relationships" is a claim about consequences for relationships, while the argument's conclusion is about healing the patient — healing and improving relationships are related but not identical goals. The argument needs the assumption that internal-only treatment cannot achieve healing, not a specific claim about whether internal-focused therapy affects relationships. This answer addresses a tangential consequence rather than the core logical link between evidence and conclusion.
73	 -- explanation style=brief
74	   step0 [Conclusion] To help a patient heal, the psychotherapist must focus on the need for positive change in the patient's relationships with other people.
75	   step1 [Evidence] The patient's troubles do not have purely internal causes — they result in part from the patient's relationships with others.
76	   step2 [Evaluate] The argument moves from "troubles are partly relational" to "therapist must focus on relationships." This assumes that addressing only internal causes would be insufficient for healing. If internal-only treatment could heal the patient despite the relational component, the conclusion would not follow.
77	   step3 [Goal] Find the necessary assumption: that healing cannot be achieved by addressing only internal causes, making the relational focus essential.
78	   note A: The argument discusses patients' own relationships, not "the troubles of other people." Additionally, the claim about therapists being unable to change relationships is stronger and different from what the argument assumes.
79	   note B: Some patients not being healed despite relational change does not establish why relational focus is necessary. The argument needs the assumption that internal-only treatment is insufficient, not that relational treatment sometimes fails.
80	   note C: This guarantees that relational change produces healing, but the argument only needs relational focus to be necessary, not sufficient. The argument survives the negation of this claim: even if relational change does not always work, it could still be a required focus area.
81	   note D: If internal-only treatment cannot heal patients, then relational focus becomes necessary. Negate it: "a therapist CAN heal by addressing only internal causes." This destroys the conclusion, confirming the assumption is necessary.
82	   note E: This discusses the consequences of internal-only therapy for relationships, but the argument needs an assumption about why relational focus is necessary for healing. The connection between therapy approach and relational outcomes is tangential.
83	 -- explanation style=another_way
84	   step0 [Conclusion] Therapists MUST focus on the patient's relationships. No choice about it.
85	   step1 [Evidence] The patient's problems are not all in their head — they partly come from their relationships with other people.
86	   step2 [Evaluate] The jump here: "Problems have relational causes, so therapy must address relationships." But does it? Maybe a therapist could help a patient heal entirely by working on internal responses — building coping skills, changing thought patterns — without ever touching the relationships themselves. The argument needs an assumption that internal-only treatment would not be enough. Otherwise, why would the relational focus be mandatory?
87	   step3 [Goal] Find the assumption that makes the relational focus necessary: internal-only treatment cannot heal the patient.
88	   note A: "Focus on the troubles of other people" — wait, what? The argument is about patients working on their OWN relationships, not becoming amateur therapists for everyone else. And the "cannot help change" part is a claim about what is impossible, which is way more extreme than what the argument needs. The argument just needs "internal-only treatment is not enough for healing." This answer is talking about a different thing and saying too much about it.
89	   note B: Some patients cannot be healed even if their relationships improve? That is a bummer for those patients, but it does not answer the question the argument needs answered. The argument needs to know WHY therapy must focus on relationships — and the answer is that internal-only treatment is not enough. Knowing that relationship-focused therapy also sometimes fails is a completely separate issue. It is like asking "why do I need to eat?" and being told "even people who eat sometimes get sick." True, but irrelevant to why eating is necessary.
90	   note C: This answer promises a guarantee: change your relationships, find relief. But the argument never makes that promise. The argument says therapy MUST focus on relationships — it does not say doing so will definitely work. Flip this answer: "some people change their relationships and still have troubles." Does the argument fall apart? No — the therapist's argument is about what therapy needs to include, not about what therapy can guarantee. Necessity is not the same as a guarantee, and this answer confuses the two.
91	   note D: The argument says therapists MUST focus on relationships. That "must" is doing a lot of work — it means there is no other way. But why? Because if a therapist could heal a patient by working only on internal stuff, then the relational focus would not be mandatory. This answer locks that door: nobody can heal a patient through internal-only treatment. If that is true, the therapist has no choice but to address the relationships. Try the negation: "Actually, a therapist CAN heal someone by just working on internal stuff." If that were possible, "must focus on relationships" would become "maybe focus on relationships if you feel like it." The argument crumbles. So this assumption is necessary — it is what makes "must" the right word.
92	   note E: This answer says "focusing on internal stuff will not fix relationship problems." Okay, but that is not quite what the argument needs. The argument is about healing the patient, not specifically about fixing their relationships. These overlap, but they are not the same thing. A patient could heal (feel better, function well) without their relationships being directly improved, or vice versa. The argument needs the assumption that internal-only therapy cannot heal — not the claim that internal-only therapy cannot improve relationships. Close, but it is aiming at the wrong target.
93

143	=== PT158-S3-Q17 type=Principle-Conform EXCEPT key=D
144	STIM: Situation: A physical therapist wants her patients to derive more enjoyment from the challenge of developing physical skills. She also wants them to spend more time practicing those skills.
145	
146	Analysis: Success in meeting the first objective will bring about success in meeting the second.
147	STEM: The analysis provided for the situation above could be appropriate for each of the following situations except:
148	  (A) [Applicable] A math teacher wants her students to understand the mathematical principles taught in her course and wants them to apply these principles routinely in everyday life.
149	  (B) [Applicable] A software manufacturer wants its customers to be more satisfied with its product, and it wants them to place fewer calls to the service representative about how to use the product.
150	  (C) [Applicable] A librarian wants fewer of the books borrowed from the library to be lost or stolen, and he wants more of the library's books to be returned on time.
151	  (D) [Correct] A hardware retail company wants to construct a new, larger warehouse and wants its employees to help plan how the old warehouse will be expanded to meet any need for more space.
152	  (E) [Applicable] A concert series director wants to present a more varied repertoire and wants to attract new patrons.
153	 -- explanation style=standard
154	   step0 [Situation] You want your PT patient to better enjoy the challenge of developing physical skills. You also want them to spend more time practicing those physical skills.
155	   step1 [Analysis] If you succeed in getting them to enjoy the challenge more, it will cause them to spend more time practicing.
156	   step2 [Evaluate / Goal] It looks like we should be wanting situations where you want two things, and those two things would allow us to say ... "You know what ... If you get that first thing you want, it will cause that second thing to happen".
157	   note A: The teacher wants students to (1) understand mathematical principles and (2) enjoy learning math. The analysis pattern applies: if students understand the principles being taught (objective 1), they will naturally enjoy the learning experience more (objective 2). Understanding creates engagement, which creates enjoyment. This mirrors the original: achieving a cognitive/experiential improvement (understanding/enjoyment) leads to a behavioral outcome (enjoyment/more time). The causal link between comprehension and enjoyment is a standard motivational connection — people tend to enjoy activities they understand and can succeed at. This fits the pattern.
158	   note B: The software manufacturer wants customers to (1) be more satisfied with its product and (2) have customers recommend the product to others. The analysis pattern applies: if customers are more satisfied (objective 1), they will naturally recommend the product to others (objective 2). Satisfied customers become advocates — this is the core logic of word-of-mouth marketing. The causal link is direct and intuitive: positive experience leads to positive recommendation. This perfectly mirrors the original's pattern where an internal state improvement (satisfaction/enjoyment) leads to a desired behavioral outcome (recommendations/more practice time).
159	   note C: The librarian wants (1) fewer borrowed books to be lost or stolen and (2) borrowers to return books in good condition. The analysis pattern applies: if fewer books are lost or stolen (objective 1), borrowers are treating books more responsibly overall, which means they will also return books in better condition (objective 2). Both objectives are symptoms of the same underlying behavior — responsible handling of borrowed materials. Fostering the care that prevents loss and theft (objective 1) naturally extends to the care that preserves book condition (objective 2). The causal mechanism is about a general improvement in responsibility that manifests in multiple positive outcomes.
160	   note D: The hardware company wants (1) to construct a new, larger warehouse and (2) its employees to start working on Saturdays. These two objectives have no natural causal connection. Building a bigger warehouse does not cause employees to be willing to work Saturdays. Unlike every other answer, where achieving the first objective creates the conditions for the second, these two goals are completely independent. A new warehouse is a physical infrastructure project; Saturday work is a labor scheduling decision. There is no mechanism by which constructing a building leads to employees agreeing to work an extra day. The company would need separate strategies for each goal. This breaks the pattern where "success in meeting the first objective brings about success in meeting the second."
161	   note E: The concert series director wants (1) to present a more varied repertoire and (2) to attract new audience members. The analysis pattern applies: if the concert series offers more varied programming (objective 1), it will naturally attract new audience members who are drawn to the expanded offerings (objective 2). Variety broadens appeal — programming that previously only attracted fans of one genre now attracts fans of multiple genres. The causal link is direct: diversified content attracts a diversified audience. This mirrors the original pattern where an improvement in the offering (variety/enjoyment) leads to a desired participation outcome (new audience/more practice time).

260	=== PT112-S1-Q9 type=Paradox key=B
261	STIM: Raisins are made by drying grapes in the sun. Although some of the sugar in the grapes is caramelized in the process, nothing is added. Moreover, the only thing removed from the grapes is the water that evaporates during the drying, and water contains no calories or nutrients. The fact that raisins contain more iron per calorie than grapes do is thus puzzling.
262	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to explain why raisins contain more iron per calorie than do grapes?
263	  (A) [Reinforces Surprise] Since grapes are bigger than raisins, it takes several bunches of grapes to provide the same amount of iron as a handful of raisins does.
264	  (B) [Correct] Caramelized sugar cannot be digested, so its calories do not count toward the calorie content of raisins.
265	  (C) [Out of Scope: rate of absorption] The body can absorb iron and other nutrients more quickly from grapes than from raisins because of the relatively high water content of grapes.
266	  (D) [Reinforces Surprise] Raisins, but not grapes, are available year-round, so many people get a greater share of their yearly iron intake from raisins than from grapes.
267	  (E) [Out of Scope: accompanying foods] Raisins are often eaten in combination with other iron-containing foods, while grapes are usually eaten by themselves.
268	 -- explanation style=single
269	   step0 [Given that] Raisins are made from grapes, and nothing is added in the process,
270	   step1 [Why is it that] Raisins contain more iron per calorie than do grapes?
271	   step2 [Evaluate] There must be something about the process of going from a grape to a raisin that increases the iron per calorie.
272	A ratio like Iron/Calorie can be increased two ways: increase the numerator (add more iron) or decrease the denominator (subtract calories).
273	The facts we're provided about the process of going from grape to raisin are these: - some sugar is caramelized - nothing is added - water evaporates (but water contains no calories)
274	   step3 [Goal] Since there's no way to add more iron, we want an answer to talk to us about how going from a grape to a raisin sheds calories.
275	   note A: This reinforces the fact that raisins are more iron-rich than grapes (you'd only need a handful of raisins to get the same iron you'd get out of eating several bunches of grapes).
276	
277	But it doesn't explain **why** raisins are more iron-rich than grapes.
278	   note B: This is offering us a story of how a grape sheds calories en route to being a raisin.
279	
280	The grape had 20mg of iron and 10 calories. **2 mg iron per calorie**
281	
282	The raisin keeps that 20mg of iron. "The only thing removed from the grapes is water".
283	
284	But the sugar in the grape caramelizes as it becomes a raisin. And when we measure how many calories something has, we don't count calories in caramelized sugar (because the body can't digest it).
285	
286	So when we measure the raisin that formed from that 10 calorie grape, we get a number lower than 10.
287	
288	Some of the grape's 10 calories were from sugar. When that sugar caramelized, then those sugar-calories stop being counted.
289	
290	If there were 3 calories from the sugar, then now that the grape has caramelized into a raisin, that 10 calorie grape will only be a 7 calorie raisin.
291	
292	And if the raisin has 20mg of iron and is only 7 calories, then it's close to being **3 mg iron per capita**.
293	
294	So this answer allows us to explain why a raisin made from a given grape can have more iron per calorie than the grape, even though no iron is added and only water is removed.
295	   note C: We're trying to explain why a raisin has more iron per calorie than the grape it formed from.
296	
297	The paradox is only about the chemistry of the grape/raisin. It has nothing to do with organisms that consume grapes or raisins. So it's irrelevant how a grape or raisin interacts with a human body.
298	   note D: This reinforces the fact that raisins are more iron-rich than grapes (people get more of their yearly iron from raisins than from grapes).
299	
300	But it doesn't explain **why** raisins are more iron-rich than grapes.
301	   note E: We're trying to explain why a raisin has more iron per calorie than the grape from which it was formed.
302	
303	The paradox is only about the chemistry of the grape/raisin. It has nothing to do with any other foods you might later mix with raisins or grapes. This answer feels like a correct answer, because it feels like we're offering an alternate explanation for why raisins seem to have more iron than grapes. ("It's not because raisins are inherently more iron-heavy, it's just that they're eaten with other foods that supply iron.")
304	
305	But we are accepting as a given the fact that raising are more iron-heavy than grapes. We need to explain why they are so.
306

395	=== PT116-S3-Q6 type=Agree/Disagree key=E
396	STIM: Antonia: The stock market is the best place to invest your money these days; although it is often volatile, it provides the opportunity to make a large profit quickly. Maria: I agree that the stock market provides the opportunity to make large profits quickly, but one is just as likely to take a huge loss. I think it is better to invest in savings bonds, which provide a constant, reliable income over many years.
397	STEM: Antonia's and Maria's statements provide the most support for holding that they disagree about whether
398	  (A) [Both Agree] the stock market is often volatile but provides the opportunity to make a large profit quickly
399	  (B) [Nobody Agrees] savings bonds can provide a large return on one’s investment
400	  (C) [Nobody Disagrees] the stock market provides the opportunity for an investor to make a constant, reliable income over many years
401	  (D) [Nobody Disagrees] it is safer to invest in savings bonds than to invest in the stock market
402	  (E) [Correct] it is preferable to pick an investment offering a reliable income over a riskier opportunity to make a large profit quickly
403	 -- explanation style=single
404	   step0 [Antonia's Position] The stock market is the best place to invest. It is volatile, but it gives you a shot at a large, quick profit.
405	   step1 [Maria's Position] Yes, you can make a large profit quickly, but you are just as likely to take a huge loss. Savings bonds are better, because they pay a constant, reliable income.
406	   step2 [Evaluate] Look at what Maria concedes. Maria agrees about the quick profits, and Antonia already admitted the volatility. So the facts are shared. What they disagree about is the tradeoff: Antonia picks the big upside despite the risk; Maria picks the steady income. Antonia never mentions "reliable income" by name, but calling the volatile market the *best* place to invest puts Antonia on the other side of that choice.
407	   step3 [Goal] Whether a reliable income is better than a risky chance at a fast, large profit.
408	   note A: Speaker 1 says this in her second and third claims. Speaker 2 affirms this in her first claim.
409	   note B: Neither speaker claims that a bond can provide a large return. Speaker 2 says bonds provide income that is both constant and reliable, but the amount of return relative to the investment isn't implied.
410	   note C: Speaker two definitely agrees, but Speaker 1 doesn't necessarily disagree.
411	   note D: Speaker two definitely agrees, but Speaker 1 doesn't necessarily disagree.
412	   note E: This one might seem at first to be a "half scope" answer (meaning 1 speaker doesn't address one of the major concepts), but it's the best that we've got. True, speaker 1 doesn't explicitly address the concept of "reliable income," but when she says that the volatile stock market is the best way to invest despite its volatility because of its big potential payoff, that lends strong support to the idea that she'd disagree that it' preferable to pick a reliable investment over a riskier one with a bigger potential payoff.

200	=== PT130-S1-Q24 type=Weaken key=E
201	STIM: The Kiffer Forest Preserve, in the northernmost part of the Abbimac Valley, is where most of the bears in the valley reside. During the eight years that the main road through the preserve has been closed the preserve's bear population has nearly doubled. Thus, the valley's bear population will increase if the road is kept closed.
202	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most undermines the argument?
203	  (A) [Opposite / Unclear Impact] Most of the increase in the preserve's bear population over the past eight years is due to migration.
204	  (B) [Opposite / Unclear Impact] Only some of the increase in the preserve's bear population over the past eight years is due to migration of bears from other parts of the Abbimac Valley.
205	  (C) [Opposite / Unclear Impact] Only some of the increase in the preserve's bear population over the past eight years is due to migration of bears from outside the Abbimac Valley.
206	  (D) [Unclear Impact / Too Weak] The bear population in areas of the Abbimac Valley outside the Kiffer Forest Preserve has decreased over the past eight years.
207	  (E) [Correct] The bear population in the Abbimac Valley has remained about the same over the past eight years.
208	 -- explanation style=single
209	   step0 [Conclusion] If the road through the preserve is kept close, the Valley's bear population will increase.
210	   step1 [Evidence] During the eight years that the road has been closed, the population of bears inside the Kiffer Preserve has nearly doubled. Most of the bears in the Valley live in this Preserve.
211	   step2 [Evaluation] The author is making a causal assumption. She she's that the preserve's population of bears has nearly doubled while the road has been closed and then assumes that "the road being closed is causing there to be more bears there". Maybe, but there could be other ways to explain the uptick in bear population, so we could weaken the argument by coming up with a different way to explain why the bear population has gone up. The author is also shifting from talking about a population increase in the preserve to talking about a population increase in the valley. We might want to think about the difference there. Maybe the population goes up in the preserve (which is in the valley) but goes down in other parts of the valley. Finally, the author is making a prediction. Even if we granted that the road closure was the causal reason for the uptick in bears, there could be factors now that are different from 8 years ago. Maybe something meaningful has changed where further road closures would not have continuing effect on increasing the bear population (maybe they have a max population limited by the size of that habitat).
212	   step3 [Goal] There are lots of attack points. Let's focus on the anti-conclusion and look for a way to argue that "we could keep the road closed, but it wouldn't lead to an increase in the valley's bear population."
213	   note A: This may seem to weaken the author's causal assumption that the road closure is boosting the bear population, because it seems to provide an alternative explanation: the uptick isn't because of the road closure, it's because of migration. However, is that an alternative explanation or part of the same causal story?
214	Author could say: having this road closed is creating a tranquil, undisturbed natural preserve, which attracts bears. Look, over the last 8 years, we have tons of new bears in this preserve, and (according to this answer) they didn't come from other parts of the valley, they migrated here from elsewhere! If we keep the road closed, we'll see more of that.
215	As we'll see in correct answer E, if the new bears coming to the preserve were just coming from elsewhere in the valley, then they wouldn't be raising the total number of bears in the valley. It would be the same valley bear population as before, just re-shuffled into different areas. So being told that the new bears are from outside the valley strengthens the author's case. This answer doesn't clarify whether these bears have migrated from elsewhere in the valley or from outside the valley.
216	   note B: For all the reasons cited in the explanation for choice A, this also strengthens. By saying "only some new bears came from other parts of the valley", we learn that "some new bears came from outside the valley", which helps the author argue that the valley's bear population has increased and will continue to do so.
217	   note C: This has an identical impact to B, so neither answer could be correct. They're both using the formulation "only some", which reveals that "some of the increase came from outside the valley, some from inside the valley". The ones coming from outside the valley strengthen the author's case. The ones coming from inside the Valley (if they are outside the Preserve) would weaken the author's case.
218	   note D: This looks tempting: if the population in the preserve is going up, but in the area of the valley that's outside of the preserve it's going down, that looks like bears are traveling from other parts of the valley into the preserve.
219	That scenario weakens the argument: it explains how the population in the preserve is climbing while allowing for the valley's bear population to have stayed the same overall.
220	But is this answer actually saying that the bears have gone from the non-preserve parts of the valley to the preserve? No, we're just adding that. This answer ends up being less convincing than the best answer. Because this answer is at least suggestive of an alternate explanation that would hurt the author's argument (population increase in preserve is just from other parts of the valley), this could be a correct answer on a Weaken question. But it's not the best available answer in this case: E is way stronger at delivering the same objection.
221	   note E: If the valley's total population has stayed the same for the past eight years during the road closure, that means two things:
222	1. the increase in bears in the preserve must have been accompanied by a decrease in bears in the non-preserve parts of the valley (maybe they migrated over, or maybe the death rate went up in the non-preserve parts while the birth-rate went up inside the preserve)
223	2. the author no longer has his correlation to stand on: during those 8 years of road closure, the preserve's population increased. But during those 8 years of road closure, the valley's population did not increase. So why should we believe the conclusion that if we keep the road closed, we'll see a population increase in the valley? If we haven't seen one yet, why would we expect one now?
224

160	STIM: Speaker: Like many contemporary critics, Smith argues that the true meaning of an author's statements can be understood only through insight into the author's social circumstances. But this same line of analysis can be applied to Smith's own words. This, in turn, suggests that Smith herself is not aware of the true meaning of her own words.

162	  (A) [Out of Scope] Insight into the intended meaning of an author's work is not as important as insight into its true meaning.

164	  (C) [Out of Scope] There is just one meaning that Smith intends her work to have.
165	  (D) [Term Shift] Smith's theory about the relation of social circumstances to the understanding of meaning lacks insight.
166	  (E) [Out of Scope] The intended meaning of an author's work is not always good evidence of its true meaning.

173	   note C: The meaning intended by Smith is not relevant to the argument.

328	=== PT133-S2-Q19 type=Must Be True key=D
329	STIM: Style manual: Archaic spellings and styles of punctuation in direct quotations from older works are to be preserved if they occur infrequently and do not interfere with a reader's comprehension. However, if they occur frequently, the editor may modernize them, inserting a note with an explanation to this effect in the text, or if similar modernizing has been done in more than one quotation, inserting a general statement in the preface. On the other hand, obvious typographical errors in quotations from modern works may be corrected without explanation.
330	STEM: Which one of the following follows logically from the statements above?
331	  (A) [Contradiction] If an editor corrects the spelling of a quoted word and the word occurs only once in the text, then an explanation should appear in a note or in the text.
332	  (B) [Contradiction] An editor may modernize an archaic spelling of a word found in a modern work without providing an explanation.
333	  (C) [Negation] An editor should modernize an archaic spelling of a word that is quoted from an older work if the spelling interferes with reader comprehension.
334	  (D) [Correct] An editor may modernize punctuation directly quoted from an older work if that punctuation occurs frequently and interferes with reader comprehension.
335	  (E) [Too Strong] If an editor modernizes only one of several similar instances of quoted archaic punctuation, an explanation should appear in the preface of the work.
336	 -- explanation style=single
337	   step0 [Given] Archaic spellings and styles of punctuation in direct quotations from older works are to be preserved if they occur infrequently and do not interfere with a reader’s comprehension. However, if they occur frequently, the editor may modernize them, inserting a note with an explanation to this effect in the text, or if similar modernizing has been done in more than one quotation, inserting a general statement in the preface. On the other hand, obvious typographical errors in quotations from modern works may be corrected without explanation.
338	   note A: The third statement indicates that spelling may be corrected without explanation.
339	   note B: From the first and second statements the editor should preserve the archaic spelling of a quoted word or provide an explanation if it is modernized.
340	   note C: This negates the first statement. ~F + IRC → ~P
341	   note D: This answer provides more F + IRC → MM
342	than enough information to
343	trigger the second statement.
344	   note E: The second part of the second statement implies that if such modernizing has been done in more than one quotation, a statement in the preface should be included. But to say that is true if such modernizing has been done in only one of several similar instances goes beyond what can be inferred.
345

372	STEM: Which one of the following is a point of disagreement between Meli and Sam?

375	  (C) [Unsupported] The preservation of a predatory species is easier to ensure than the preservation of the species preyed upon.

Intervening is the opposite of "letting nature take its course". So let's look for an answer about "whether or not we should intervene and try to protect the bighorns", or "whether or not we should let nature take its course and just hope the bighorns survive".

2	STIM: Editorial: Many critics of consumerism insist that advertising persuades people that they need certain consumer goods when they merely desire them. However, this accusation rests on a fuzzy distinction, that between wants and needs. In life, it is often impossible to determine whether something is merely desirable or whether it is essential to one's happiness.
3	STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses the conclusion drawn in the editorial's argument?
4	  (A) [Correct] The claim that advertising persuades people that they need things that they merely want rests on a fuzzy distinction.
5	  (B) [Unsupported] Many critics of consumerism insist that advertising attempts to blur people's ability to distinguish between wants and needs.
6	  (C) [Unsupported] There is nothing wrong with advertising that tries to persuade people that they need certain consumer goods.
7	  (D) [Unsupported] Many critics of consumerism fail to realize that certain things are essential to human happiness.
8	  (E) [Too Strong] Critics of consumerism often use fuzzy distinctions to support their claims.
9	 -- explanation style=single
10	   step0 [Opposing Point] Many critics of consumerism insist that advertising persuades people that they need certain consumer goods when they merely desire them.
11	   step1 [Evidence] In life, it is often impossible to determine whether something is merely desirable or whether it is essential to one’s happiness.
12	   step2 [Conclusion] This accusation rests on a fuzzy distinction, that between wants and needs.
13	   note A: This best paraphrases the argument’s main point.
14	   note B: While critics of consumerism insist that advertising persuades people that they need certain consumer goods, it does not attempt to blur people’s ability to distinguish between wants and needs.
15	   note C: This point is not made in the argument.
16	   note D: This point is not made in the argument.
17	   note E: The main point of the argument is that critics used a fuzzy distinction to support their claim in this case. However, that does not mean that critics of consumerism often use fuzzy distinctions.
18

22	 OLD: While critics of consumerism insist that advertising persuades people that they need certain consumer goods, it does not attempt to blur people’s ability to distinguish between wants and needs.
23	 NEW: The critics insist that advertising persuades people they need things they merely want. The editorial never says the critics accuse advertising of trying to blur people's ability to tell wants from needs. And even an accurate summary of the critics' view would be the opposing point, not the editorial's conclusion.
24	 WHY: The note asserted, without support, that advertising 'does not attempt to blur' the distinction; B fails because it misstates the critics' claim, and a critics' claim would not be the conclusion anyway.
25	 EVID: B: 'Many critics of consumerism insist that advertising attempts to blur people's ability to distinguish between wants and needs.' Stimulus: 'Many critics of consumerism insist that advertising persuades people that they need certain consumer goods when they merely desire them.'

27	=== PT134-S1-Q1 type=Main Conclusion key=C
28	STIM: Sometimes it is advisable for a medical patient to seek a second opinion. But this process can be awkward for both the patient and the physicians, since the patient often worries that the first physician will be alienated. In addition, for the first physician there is the issue of pride: a second opinion tacitly highlights a physician's fallibility. And the second physician is in the position of evaluating not only a patient's health, but also, inevitably and uncomfortably, a colleague's work.
29	STEM: Which one of the following most accurately states the conclusion of the argument as a whole?
30	  (A) [Term Shift] Because of the awkwardness involved, it is best for patients not to seek second opinions unless it is absolutely necessary.
31	  (B) [Term Shift] In cases in which second opinions are necessary, the first physician often feels that his or her professional judgment is called into question.
32	  (C) [Correct] The process of obtaining a second medical opinion can be awkward for those involved.
33	  (D) [Evidence] Physicians who are called upon to offer second opinions are always uncomfortable about evaluating the work of colleagues.
34	  (E) [Evidence] In many cases in which medical patients seek second opinions, they are concerned about offending the first physician.
35	 -- explanation style=single
36	   step0 [Evidence] The patient often worries that the first physician will be alienated. In addition, for the first physician there is the issue of pride: a second opinion tacitly highlights a physician’s fallibility. And the second physician is in the position of evaluating not only a patient’s health, but also, inevitably and uncomfortably, a colleague’s work.
37	   step1 [Conclusion] This process can be awkward for both the patient and the physicians.
38	   note A: Advisable is not the same as, nor the equivalent of, necessary.
39	   note B: Advisable is not the same as, nor the equivalent of, necessary.
40	   note C: This best paraphrases the argument’s main point.
41	   note D: This is a premise of the argument.
42	   note E: This is a premise of the argument.
43

52	=== PT134-S2-Q17 type=Sufficient Assumption key=E
53	STIM: People want to be instantly and intuitively liked. Those persons who are perceived as forming opinions of others only after cautiously gathering and weighing the evidence are generally resented. Thus, it is imprudent to appear prudent.
54	STEM: Which one of the following, if assumed, enables the argument's conclusion to be properly drawn?
55	  (A) [Negated Premise] People who act spontaneously are well liked.
56	  (B) [Out of Scope] Imprudent people act instantly and intuitively.
57	  (C) [Too Weak] People resent those less prudent than themselves.
58	  (D) [Out of Scope] People who are intuitive know instantly when they like someone.
59	  (E) [Correct] It is imprudent to cause people to resent you.
60	 -- explanation style=single
61	   step0 [Evidence] Those persons who are AP → R perceived as forming opinions of others only after cautiously gathering and weighing the evidence are generally resented.
62	   step1 [Conclusion] It is imprudent to appear AP → ~P prudent.
63	   step2 [Evaluate] The argument assumes R → ~P that it is not prudent to be resented.
64	   note A: This answer negates the ~AP → ~R
65	premise of the argument.
66	   note B: This answer is about how imprudent people act, which is irrelevant to this argument about how people appear.
67	   note C: This answer doesn’t go so far as suggesting that people resent those who are imprudent. While someone may be less prudent than someone else, they still may be prudent. The opposite is also true—someone may be more prudent than someone else and yet still be imprudent.
68	   note D: Knowing when someone likes another is not relevant to this argument.
69	   note E: This links being resented R → ~P
70	with being imprudent.
71

111	=== PT134-S3-Q3 type=Most Strongly Supported key=C
112	STIM: Engine noise from boats traveling through killer whales’ habitats ranges in frequency from 100 hertz to 3,000 hertz, an acoustical range that overlaps that in which the whales communicate through screams and squeals. Though killer whales do not seem to behave differently around running boat engines, engine noise from boats can be loud enough to damage their hearing over time. Therefore, _______ .
113	STEM: Which one of the following most logically completes the argument?
114	  (A) [Contradiction] younger killer whales are better able to tolerate engine noise from boats than older whales are
115	  (B) [Out of Scope] killer whales are less likely to attempt to communicate with one another when boat engines are operating nearby
116	  (C) [Correct] noise from boat engines may impair killer whales’ ability to communicate
117	  (D) [Out of Scope] killer whales are most likely to prefer areas where boat traffic is present, but light
118	  (E) [Out of Scope] killer whales would probably be more successful in finding food if boats did not travel through their habitats
119	 -- explanation style=single
120	   step0 [Given] Engine noise from boats traveling through killer whales’ habitats ranges in frequency from 100 hertz to 3,000 hertz, an acoustical range that overlaps that in which the whales communicate through screams and squeals. Though killer whales do not seem to behave differently around running boat engines, engine noise from boats can be loud
121	   note A: If engine noise damages whales’ hearing over time, it’s more likely that older whales would be more able to tolerate engine noise, since they would be less likely to hear it in the first place.
122	   note B: When killer whales attempt to communicate is not discussed in the statements.
123	   note C: Since engine noise from boats can damage killer whales’ hearing, it may impair their ability to communicate.
124	   note D: The areas preferred by killer whales is not discussed in the statements.
125	   note E: How successful whales would be in finding food is not discussed in the statements.
126

141	=== PT134-S3-Q7 type=Agree/Disagree key=B
142	STIM: Tanner: The public should demand political debates before any election. Voters are better able to choose the candidate best suited for office if they watch the candidates seriously debate one another. Saldana: Political debates almost always benefit the candidate who has the better debating skills. Thus, they don't really help voters determine which candidate is most qualified for office.
143	STEM: The dialogue provides the most support for the claim that Tanner and Saldana disagree over which one of the following?
144	  (A) [Too Strong] Political candidates with strong debating skills are more likely to win elections than those with weak debating skills.
145	  (B) [Correct] A voter who watches a political debate will likely be better able, as a result, to determine which candidate is more qualified for office.
146	  (C) [Half Scope] Debating skills are of little use to politicians in doing their jobs once they are elected to office.
147	  (D) [Half Scope] The candidates with the best debating skills are the ones who are most qualified for the political offices for which they are running.
148	  (E) [Too Strong] Political debates tend to have a major effect on which candidate among those participating in a debate will win the election.
149	 -- explanation style=single

Voters are better Thus, they don’t able to choose really help voters the candidate best determine which suited for office if candidate is most they watch the qualified for office. candidates seriously debate one another.
151	   step1 [Evaluate] Tanner and Saldana disagree about whether watching candidates debate one another helps voters determine who is best suited for office.
152	   note A: Neither Tanner nor Saldana would agree with this statement.
153	   note B: Tanner would agree with this statement, while Saldana would disagree with it.
154	   note C: Saldana would agree with this statement, but Tanner does not address it.
155	   note D: Saldana would disagree with this statement, but Tanner does not address it.
156	   note E: Neither Tanner nor Saldana would agree with this statement.

174	=== PT134-S3-Q26 type=Sufficient Assumption key=E
175	STIM: Acquiring complete detailed information about all the pros and cons of a product one might purchase would clearly be difficult and expensive. It is rational not to acquire such information unless one expects that the benefits of doing so will outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so. Therefore, consumers who do not bother to acquire such information are thereby behaving rationally.
176	STEM: The conclusion of the argument is properly drawn if which one of the following is assumed?
177	  (A) [Reversal] Rational consumers who do not expect that the benefits outweigh the cost and difficulty of acquiring detailed information about a product they might purchase usually do not bother to acquire such information.
178	  (B) [Negation] Whenever it is rational not to acquire detailed information about a product, it would be irrational to bother to acquire such information.
179	  (C) [Too Weak] The benefits of acquiring detailed information about a product one might purchase usually do not outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so.
180	  (D) [Negation] Rational consumers usually expect that the benefits of acquiring detailed information about a product they might purchase would not outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so.
181	  (E) [Correct] Consumers who do not bother to acquire complete detailed information about a product they might purchase do not expect that the benefits of acquiring such information will outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so.
182	 -- explanation style=single
183	   step0 [Evidence] Acquiring complete detailed ACDI → D + E information about all the pros and cons of a product one might purchase would clearly be difficult and expensive. It is rational not to acquire ~R → BOC such information unless one expects that the benefits of doing so will outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so.
184	   step1 [Conclusion] Therefore, consumers who ~ACDI → R do not bother to acquire such information are thereby behaving rationally.
185	   step2 [Evaluate] The argument assumes that ~ACDI → ~BOC people who do not bother to acquire complete detailed information about all the pros and cons of a product do not believe the benefits of doing so will outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so.
186	   note A: This reverses the R + ~BOC → ~ACDI
187	assumption of
188	the argument.
189	   note B: This negates the ACDI → ~R
190	argument’s conclusion.
191	   note C: While this indicates that the benefits to a consumer of acquiring detailed information about a product one might purchase do not typically outweigh the costs of acquiring such information, it does not establish what the consumer expects about the benefits of acquiring such information.
192	   note D: This negates the second R → ~BOC
193	premise in the argument.
194	   note E: This bridges the gap ~ACDI → ~BOC
195	between the second
196	premise and the
197	conclusion.

261	STIM: Any museum that owns the rare stamp that features an airplane printed upside down should not display it. Ultraviolet light causes red ink to fade, and a substantial portion of the stamp is red. If the stamp is displayed, it will be damaged. It should be kept safely locked away, even though this will deny the public the chance to see it.
262	STEM: The reasoning above most closely conforms to which one of the following principles?
263	  (A) [Out of Scope] The public should judge the quality of a museum by the rarity of the objects in its collection.
264	  (B) [Weaken] Museum display cases should protect their contents from damage caused by ultraviolet light.
265	  (C) [Out of Scope] Red ink should not be used on items that will not be exposed to ultraviolet light.
266	  (D) [Correct] A museum piece that would be damaged by display should not be displayed.
267	  (E) [Weaken] The primary purpose of a museum is to educate the public.

273	   note B: This finds an alternative solution to the problem and might suggest that if the museum could acquire the display case that it should display the stamp.
274	   note C: The argument is about whether to display the stamp, while this answer is about whether to use red ink on the production of items that might occur in the future.

Weaken A
To test the claim that vitamin C is effective in treating acne, scientists administered it to one group of subjects and a placebo to a control group. The group receiving vitamin C had less severe acne during the study than did the control group. It was subsequently discovered, however, that half of the subjects in each group knew which kind of pill they were given. Among those who could not tell, no difference in the severity of acne was found between the two groups. Therefore, we can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne.
Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the argument?
A Correct The subjects who were given vitamin C had a history of suffering from more severe acne than did the subjects receiving a placebo.
B No Impact None of the subjects who were given vitamin C took additional doses of vitamin C on their own.
C No Impact During the study, the severity of the subjects' acne was lower than the national average.
D Strengthens Some of the subjects who were given placebos consumed foods during the study that are naturally rich in vitamin C.
E No Impact Some of the subjects who knew their pills were placebos did not actually take the pills they were given.
-- standard 011c47e1-9daa-4d67-8a2d-7bff9623600d If placebo subjects consumed foods naturally rich in vitamin C, this muddies the distinction between the two groups. The control group was effectively getting some vitamin C through diet, which could have reduced their acne. If the placebo group's acne was already being reduced by dietary vitamin C, then the absence of a difference between groups does not prove vitamin C is ineffective — it suggests both groups were receiving some vitamin C. However, this actually supports the argument's conclusion more than it weakens it: if both groups got vitamin C and there was no difference, then additional vitamin C through pills provides no extra benefit.
-- brief b4f8ee68-7f98-489f-9339-ff0242629f75 If the placebo group also got vitamin C through food, the lack of difference between groups still suggests the pills added no benefit. This supports the conclusion rather than weakening it.
-- another_way fa758a01-be00-4123-be04-47a3fdc10084 The placebo group was eating vitamin C-rich foods? If anything, that helps the argument. If both groups were getting vitamin C (one through pills, one through diet) and there was no difference, that is more evidence vitamin C does not help with acne. This answer accidentally supports the conclusion instead of weakening it.
